Texas 2026: what the Democratic winners have said and done on public education

Executive summary

This document records what 20 Texas Democrats have said and done on public education: James Talarico, the U.S. senator-elect; seven statewide executive winners (Gina Hinojosa, governor; Vikki Goodwin, lieutenant governor; Nathan Johnson, attorney general; Sarah Eckhardt, comptroller; Benjamin Flores, land commissioner; Clayton Tucker, agriculture commissioner; Jon Rosenthal, railroad commissioner); four Texas Supreme Court winners (Maggie Ellis, Chari Kelly, Kristen Hawkins, Gisela D. Triana); five winners for the State Board of Education, the elected 15-member board that sets curriculum standards and approves teaching materials (Thomas Ray Garcia, Coretta Mallet-Fontenot, Allison Bush, Michelle Palmer, Tiffany Clark); Dana Van de Walker, whose board race is not called; and Marisa B. Pérez-Díaz and Gustavo Reveles, sitting board Democrats who were not on the ballot.

All six who have served in the Legislature, the state's House and Senate (Hinojosa, Goodwin, Talarico, Rosenthal, Johnson and Eckhardt), voted no on Senate Bill 2 of 2025, which created the state's education savings accounts (state money a family can spend on private-school costs), at every recorded floor stage (House Journal; Senate Journal). The voucher statements found for the others who are not judges oppose public money for private-school tuition. Two statements are qualified: Bush's answer says public funds "including vouchers" must strengthen classrooms and does not call for repeal (campaign FAQ), and a Texas Tribune guide reports Hinojosa as saying vouchers "should be expanded to include public pre-K programs", under a child-care heading, without saying which vouchers it means (HPPR). Nothing in their own words was found for Clark or Van de Walker. Eckhardt, who will run the program, pledges to "audit the daylights out of" it (The Texan).

Charter schools divide them. Talarico wrote in 2022 of "a role for high-quality nonprofit public charter schools" in a survey by the Association of Texas Professional Educators (ATPE), a teacher association that does not endorse candidates, published on its Teach the Vote candidate-information site (ATPE Teach the Vote), while Goodwin, Rosenthal, Mallet-Fontenot, Palmer, Van de Walker and Reveles have asked for fewer new charters or a pause. Pérez-Díaz called for "a halt to the expansion" in 2018 and later voted to allow several new charters (ATPE Teach the Vote); Clark and Reveles voted against allowing every applicant on a named roll call in 2025 and 2026 except Unidos Soccer Leadership Academy, which each voted to allow and then voted not to veto (board minutes).

On House Bill 4545 of 2021 (retakes and extra instruction for students who fail the state test, STAAR), Talarico voted yes at every House floor vote, after voting no in committee; Goodwin and Rosenthal voted no throughout; and Hinojosa voted no and then yes on the final version (House Journal). All four House members and Johnson voted against House Bill 8 of 2025, the law replacing STAAR (House Journal).

This review found no statements on education policy by the four judges in the sources it could open, and the Code of Judicial Conduct bars pledges suggesting how they would decide classes of cases. Their record is rulings, such as Triana's 2020 opinion upholding an injunction that Houston ISD (the Houston Independent School District; "ISD" stands for independent school district) won against the commissioner of education's takeover steps. In that takeover, announced in 2019, the state put the district under "an agency-run board of managers" in place of its elected board (Teach the Vote). The Supreme Court later reversed and vacated the injunction, noting that the Legislature had since rewritten the governing law (docket).

Texas AFT, the Texas affiliate of the national American Federation of Teachers, endorsed all 14 non-judicial 2026 candidates here; the Texas State Teachers Association endorsed all but Flores, Tucker, Rosenthal and Mallet-Fontenot (TSTA list). The largest direct contributions from teacher organizations identified were $250,000 each to Hinojosa from the national committees of the American Federation of Teachers and the National Education Association (report; report). Larger sums are reported to have gone to outside groups supporting Talarico, not to his campaign: the Houston Chronicle reported that Reed Hastings gave $1,000,000 to outside groups supporting him, without naming the receiving committee (Houston Chronicle), and the Texas Tribune reported that Stephen Mandel gave $500,000 to Lone Star Rising PAC, a super PAC (a type of political action committee, or PAC) that operates independently of his campaign (Texas Tribune). Only Talarico has 2026 reform backing, from Democrats for Education Reform, a national group that endorses Democratic candidates (DFER). The Texas Home School Coalition, a home-school advocacy group, endorsed the Republican opponent in each of the 12 statewide races in this document (U.S. Senate, the seven executive offices and the four Supreme Court seats) and three Republican State Board candidates: Victoria Hinojosa (District 2), Tiffany Nelson (District 6) and Audrey Young (District 8) (coalition list). Charter Schools Now PAC, a charter-school political committee, endorsed Greg Abbott and funded four Republican State Board candidates (report).

No statement by any of the 20 on teacher residencies was found. Hinojosa has pledged to replace Commissioner of Education Mike Morath with an educator (campaign site) and said in her victory speech, "on day one, I will fire Mike Morath" (campaign transcript). No nominee has been named (Texas Tribune), and nothing was found from her on appointments to the State Board for Educator Certification, the governor-appointed board that writes teacher-certification rules, which is separate from the elected State Board of Education. In 2021 she filed a bill, House Bill 613, to make the commissioner an elected office (bill text).

Teacher preparation and certification across the slate

Texas sets teacher-certification rules through the State Board for Educator Certification, which writes the rules for certificates and for the programs that prepare teachers. The governor appoints its 11 voting members with Senate consent, plus two nonvoting members, one of them from an alternative preparation program (Education Code, chapter 21). Every rule it proposes goes to the elected State Board of Education, which may reject the rule by a two-thirds vote of members present and voting within 90 days but may not change it (§21.042). In June 2026 the board let the rules written to carry out the 2025 school-funding law, House Bill 2, take effect on a consent calendar, with no individual votes recorded (board minutes, June 26, 2026).

The rules cover the routes into teaching. The June 2026 package covered admission to preparation programs, new preparation routes established by House Bill 2, and intern and aide certificates (committee minutes, June 25, 2026). Districts can also avoid some of these rules: the Districts of Innovation law lets a district exempt itself from some state requirements, including rules on hiring certified teachers (ATPE Teach the Vote). The largest alternative-certification provider, A+ Texas Teachers (Texas Teachers of Tomorrow), was in litigation with the agency and the certification board in 2023 and 2024 over its compliance with an agreed order (Third Court docket).

17 rows

One question recurs across the record: who should control certification rules. Johnson offered the 2023 amendment to keep the State Board of Education's power to reject them, and Eckhardt voted for it (Senate Journal). Bush and Pérez-Díaz have said the board should keep that power (ATPE Teach the Vote; ATPE Teach the Vote), Palmer prefers the elected board to the appointed one (ATPE Teach the Vote), and Garcia would give the board power to amend the rules, which the law now forbids (ATPE Teach the Vote). The other recurring subject is uncertified teachers: Hinojosa, Goodwin, Rosenthal, Mallet-Fontenot, Van de Walker, Pérez-Díaz and Reveles have each addressed them, and Reveles is the only one to say they should be allowed in some cases (League of Women Voters guide, 2024). Johnson's 2024 survey answer cites the state's figure that 28.8 percent of new teachers hired in 2022-23 were uncertified (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Nothing on teacher preparation or certification was found for Benjamin Flores, Maggie Ellis or Kristen Hawkins.

20 rows

For the four judges, the absence of teacher-organization backing is the finding. The Texas AFL-CIO, a labor federation that includes teacher-union affiliates, endorsed all four on July 21, 2026, which is a labor endorsement rather than an education one (AFL-CIO list). An Austin law firm with school-district and higher-education practices gave Kelly $1,030 (report) and Triana $1,000 (report) on November 27, 2023, in an earlier Third Court cycle.

Education-oriented groups also backed the opponents. Charter Schools Now PAC endorsed Greg Abbott (Abbott campaign), and the American Federation for Children, a school-choice group, endorsed him on February 10, 2026 (Abbott campaign). The Texas Home School Coalition endorsed the Republican opponent in each of the 12 statewide races in this document: Ken Paxton (U.S. Senate), Greg Abbott, Dan Patrick, Mayes Middleton, Don Huffines, Dawn Buckingham, Nate Sheets and Bo French (the seven executive offices), and Jimmy Blacklock, James P. Sullivan, Kyle Hawkins and Brett Busby (the four Supreme Court seats). It also endorsed three Republican State Board candidates: Victoria Hinojosa (District 2, Garcia's opponent), Tiffany Nelson (District 6, Palmer's) and Audrey Young (District 8, Van de Walker's) (coalition list). Charter Schools Now PAC gave $2,500 to Victoria Hinojosa (Garcia's opponent), $1,000 to Tammie Nielsen (Mallet-Fontenot's) and $2,500 plus $32,483 in in-kind ads to Tiffany Nelson (Palmer's) (report; report), and $5,000 to Audrey Young (Van de Walker's) in April 2025 (report). It also endorsed and funded Bush's Democratic runoff opponent, Stephanie Limon Bazan (PAC report). Commit to Students PAC, a Dallas committee whose purpose was not established, gave Dan Patrick $5,000 (report).

Two groups that appear in the profiles do not endorse candidates. The Association of Texas Professional Educators (ATPE), a teacher association, says its political committee's contributions "do not constitute a public endorsement by ATPE" (ATPE-PAC); that committee gave to Hinojosa's 2026 campaign for governor, Bush's 2026 State Board runoff campaign, and Talarico's earlier Texas House campaigns, and those items appear here as contributions. Raise Your Hand Texas, a public-education advocacy group, "will not endorse candidates" (Raise Your Hand Texas); the Charles Butt Public Education PAC, which gave to Talarico's House campaigns, is a separate committee, and no link between the two was established. Texas Parent PAC does endorse, and in 2026 it endorsed only Hinojosa in the primaries (Texas Parent PAC).

Statewide executive offices

Gina Hinojosa, Governor

Called by the Associated Press for Hinojosa over Greg Abbott, the incumbent (Texas Tribune live results, Associated Press data; Texas Secretary of State unofficial results). Not certified; the state's official count (the canvass) ends December 7, 2026.

At a glance

Private-school vouchers
Pledges to "end the voucher scam"; voted no on Senate Bill 2 (2025) at both House stages
School funding and teacher pay
Pledges teacher pay "among the highest paid in the nation"; voted yes on House Bill 2 (2025)
Testing, accountability and takeovers
Pledges to "end the STAAR test" and return taken-over districts; voted no on House Bill 8 (2025) at every stage
Charter schools
2016: opposed charter organizations taking over local schools; her bills to bar charter admission by discipline history did not pass; as a trustee in 2012 voted for recommendations that included an in-district charter at Travis Heights (Austin ISD)
Curriculum, library books and religion
Voted no on the Ten Commandments bill, Senate Bill 10 (2025), and the 2023 library bill, House Bill 900
Teacher preparation and certification
2024 letter: reversing the "decline of certified teachers" should be the priority
What the office can do on education
Appoints the education commissioner and certification-board members with Senate consent; can veto budget items (Texas Tribune; Education Code, chapter 21)
Teacher-organization backing
Texas AFT and TSTA endorsements; $250,000 each from the AFT and NEA national committees, 2026 (her report)
Charter, school-choice or reform backing
None found; Charter Schools Now PAC endorsed her opponent (Abbott campaign)
Background and powers of the officeRecord by issueVoting recordEducation-oriented backersWhat was not found

Background and powers of the office

Hinojosa won an at-large seat on the Austin Independent School District board in November 2012 (KUT). She was board president from January 2015 until December 15, 2015, when she gave up the presidency to run for the Texas House (Austin ISD, January 7, 2015; Austin ISD, December 14, 2015), and she left the board in November 2016 (Austin ISD). She has represented House District 49 in Austin since January 10, 2017 (Legislative Reference Library), and she launched her campaign for governor in Brownsville on October 15, 2025 (campaign release).

  • Education roles. In 2023 she sat on the House Public Education Committee and on the Select Committee on Educational Opportunity & Enrichment, the House's 2023 interim committee on vouchers (Texas Legislature Online, 88th Legislature). She sat on Public Education again in 2025 (committee minutes, April 3, 2025). In 2019 she chaired the House Democratic Caucus Special Committee on Public Education, a working group of House Democrats (Texas Press Association).
  • The commissioner of education. The governor appoints the commissioner who runs the Texas Education Agency (TEA), the state agency that oversees public schools. The Texas Tribune reports that "Hinojosa must appoint someone and submit the name to the Texas Senate for official confirmation", which the Tribune describes as needing a two-thirds vote (Texas Tribune, November 5, 2026).
  • Budget veto and the State Board of Education. According to the same Tribune article, the governor can veto single budget items, such as funding for the education savings account program, and names the chair of the elected State Board of Education from among its members (Texas Tribune, November 5, 2026).
  • Educator certification. Under section 21.033 of the Education Code, the governor appoints, with Senate consent, the 11 voting members of the State Board for Educator Certification (four public-school teachers, two administrators, one counselor and four citizens). The governor also appoints two nonvoting members: a dean of a college of education and a person from an alternative educator preparation program (Education Code, chapter 21). That board sets the rules for teacher certification and for the programs that prepare teachers (Education Code, chapter 21).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

Accounts checked: her Bluesky account, @ginafortexas.bsky.social, read in full from November 2024 to November 5, 2026 (471 original posts, 128 on education). Her X accounts @GinaHinojosaTX and @GinaForAustin could not be read (X returned an access error). Instagram and Facebook were not retrieved because they require a login. All posts below are from Bluesky.

Social media

No Bluesky post on charter schools, certification rules, library books or the Ten Commandments was found.

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

Her campaign site says "Gina will end the voucher scam and ensure Texans' taxpayer dollars go where they belong" (campaign site, page last modified August 11, 2026). The program she refers to is the education savings account program created by Senate Bill 2 in 2025, which uses state money for private-school costs (Texas Tribune, April 17, 2025). At a September 2026 press conference she said, "We cannot afford to take $4 billion to give to this unaccountable voucher scam, so I will block that" (Texas Tribune, November 5, 2026). On February 5, 2026 she told Austin PBS, "I believe we can undo vouchers", giving as her reason that some House and Senate Republicans want to undo them but "do not feel free to vote their conscience" (Austin PBS).

Statement or interview

  • 2016 ATPE survey: "I will not support any proposal to create a voucher, or a neo-vouchers program in Texas" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • October 19, 2023, after Abbott offered to add teacher pay to a special session once education savings accounts passed: "The Governor's bribe is immoral" (Washington Examiner, carrying The Center Square).
  • April 17, 2025, final House debate on Senate Bill 2: "This bill is everything that is wrong with politics" (Texas Tribune).
  • Election Day, November 3, 2026, citing her work with House Republicans as the model for her governorship: "In 2023, we stopped vouchers" (KXAN).

Social media

  • February 6, 2025: "If 5.5M public school kids must take it, so should everyone in taxpayer-funded education, including wealthy families taking a voucher" (Bluesky).
  • January 17, 2026: "every time they talk about 'school choice' they are actually taking options away from Texans" (Bluesky).

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

The campaign site pledges to "audit and terminate" vendor contracts and to "use that money to pay teachers to be among the highest paid in the nation"; it gives no dollar figure (campaign site). In her signed 2026 survey for the Association of Texas Professional Educators (ATPE), a teacher association that does not endorse candidates, she wrote, "I will also reinstate local control to allow districts more autonomy in how they spend their tax dollars" (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

As an Austin ISD trustee in 2016 she described recapture, the system that sends part of property-wealthy districts' school taxes to the state: "It's about $1,000 for the average property taxpayer in Austin that goes directly to the State of Texas to pay for public education elsewhere" (Spectrum News, May 10, 2016).

Statement or interview

  • The 2019 school finance law (House Bill 3). On April 3, 2019 she said of the bill, "To the teachers who dedicate their working lives to our kids, who for too long have been under-appreciated and unheard: We hear you, and this bill is for you" (Texas Press Association). Her campaign site says "Gina was a negotiator of the historic 2019 school finance bill" (campaign site). Texas Legislature Online does not list her on that bill's conference committee (Texas Legislature Online, 86th Legislature), and her own 2019 recap credits "the HB 3 conference committee members" while describing her role as "heading up the House Democratic Caucus' Special Committee on Public Education" (2019 newsletter, archived).
  • June 2, 2026, preferring higher base pay to the Teacher Incentive Allotment, the state's performance-based teacher pay program: "We need to do something about base pay so that we can impact all teachers" (Spectrum News).

Social media

  • January 28, 2025, on the basic allotment (the per-student base amount in the state formula): "That's how much TX needs to increase basic allotment (per student funding) just to get back to 2019 spending power", referring to $1,340 (Bluesky).
  • October 22, 2025, on Texas teachers: "they should be among the best paid in the nation" (Bluesky).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

The campaign site says "Gina will end the STAAR test and tests like it, pause the rigged A-F accountability system, and call on the Legislature to deliver a bill that creates real accountability" (campaign site). STAAR is the state's standardized test, written by TEA; the A-F system is the letter grade the state assigns each school and district (Texas Standard, October 23, 2026). The same page pledges, "Gina will return school districts that have been taken over back to their communities", and to replace Commissioner Mike Morath with an educator because "the agency that oversees our children's education should be led by someone who has taught them" (campaign site).

  • April 8, 2026, STAAR testing week: "As governor, I will end this testing madness that is hurting our kids and destroying our neighborhood schools" (campaign release).
  • September 22, 2026, at Houston ISD, which the state took over: "When I am governor, I will give Houston ISD back to the people of Houston, so that there can be accountability again" (campaign release).
  • Victory speech, November 3, 2026: "on day one, I will fire Mike Morath" and "your schools will not close" (campaign transcript).

Statement or interview

In 2016 she wrote, "Testing should not be a punitive measure used to fire teachers or to close neighborhood schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On March 31, 2023, when TEA appointed a conservator for Austin ISD over special education, she said, "I am dismayed by [TEA Commissioner Mike] Morath's decision to install a conservatorship in our school district at this time" (KUT).

  • 2025 testing law (House Bill 8): on August 26, 2025 she argued that TEA "should not be in charge of creating the test" (Texas Tribune); on September 3, 2025 she said, "This is no win. This is a terrible bill" (Texas Tribune).
  • Her campaign site says, "Last year, she successfully reduced the number of STAAR tests our kids take" (campaign site). The record supports this for one test. Her House amendment cut the testing schedule to the federal minimum; the Senate restored the other tests, and the enacted law drops only the English II end-of-course exam (amendment; enrolled bill). The Tribune wrote that "Hinojosa added a provision to get rid of the English II test" (Texas Tribune). She voted against the bill on second reading, third reading and concurrence with the Senate's version (vote table below).
  • October 23, 2026, on STAAR: "It is created by the Texas Education Agency that then decides how to grade it after the kids have already taken the test" and "We need an objective outside measure of our kids' performance" (Texas Standard). On Morath, in the same interview: "I don't have anything against the man personally, but I do believe we need an educator in that position to get us back on track" (Texas Standard).
  • November 5, 2026: the Tribune reported that "her team was "actively interviewing" people to replace Morath"; no nominee was named (Texas Tribune).

Social media

  • August 26, 2025: "the House adopted my amendment to cut the number of high stakes tests to the federal requirement" (Bluesky).

Charter schools

Statement or interview

  • December 2012, as a trustee, on ending Austin ISD's contract with the IDEA charter network: "At some point so much damage is done that it's time for a divorce" (KUT).
  • 2016 survey: "I do not support allowing charter organizations, management companies, or an achievement authority to take over local schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • 2019 newsletter, on her bill to bar charters from refusing students for discipline history: "Public schools take all kids, and so should charter schools." (archived newsletter).
  • Her campaign site says she "passed legislation to ban charter schools from discriminating against kids because of their disciplinary record" (campaign site). Her own 2025 bill on this, House Bill 1179, did not advance out of committee. A 2025 discipline law by another author, House Bill 6, removed the "discipline problems" exclusion ground while still allowing exclusion of a student who "has been convicted of a criminal offense or has a juvenile court adjudication" (enrolled House Bill 6). Whether she was an author or coauthor of House Bill 6 was not established; she voted for it on House second and third reading (vote table below).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Statement or interview

At a Tribune event in November 2021 she called "critical race theory" "a made-up issue that is brilliantly used to polarize people" (Texas Tribune). On September 22, 2026, on curriculum generated by artificial intelligence in Houston ISD: "I think that curriculum needs to be made by human beings" (campaign release).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

No pledge names a certification rule, program or funding source. The campaign site says experienced teachers "are being replaced by uncertified first-time teachers", in a passage that ties teacher departures to pay, workload and STAAR (campaign site). Her campaign newsletter of August 24, 2026 says "we have to give new educators the support and mentorship they need to become great teachers" and that she has "every intention of appointing an educator as head of TEA" (campaign Substack). Her newsletter of September 23, 2026, written against instruction led by artificial intelligence, says, "That means having certified teachers at the front of the classroom" (campaign Substack).

Statement or interview

Her most detailed statement is a signed letter of November 18, 2024 to Public Education Committee chair Brad Buckley, printed as Appendix A of the House Committee on Public Education Interim Report 2024. The appendix pages are scanned images; the wording below is from a text-recognition reading of them. She wrote that uncertified teachers had "tripled in the last 10 years" and that:

  • "The Speaker's Interim Recommendations appropriately identify "teacher certification" as a goal of our work."
  • "reversing the alarming, precipitous decline of certified teachers in Texas public schools should be the priority of the upcoming legislative session."
  • "But compensation that is dependent in large part on testing performance is problematic"
  • "A qualified, professional teacher in the classroom is foundational to a quality education."

Other statements on the subject:

  • 2016 survey: "I do not support using student scores on state standardized tests in teacher evaluations" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • October 2016, as a trustee, she asked that Austin ISD's pilots to reduce the number of ineffective teachers be built with the local teachers union: "Hinojosa asked for this measure to be developed hand-in-hand with Education Austin" (Austin Monitor).
  • May 5, 2026 newsletter: "an alarming 10 point decrease in the number of certified teachers since Greg Abbott has been governor" (no source for the figure is given) (campaign Substack).
  • August 14, 2026 newsletter, repeating the "uncertified first-time teachers" line (campaign Substack), and a September 9, 2026 campaign email with "uncertified, first-time teachers" (archived email).
  • A June 2, 2026 Spectrum News article that quotes her on base pay also states that "House Bill 2 also banned the hiring of uncertified teachers"; that description of the 2025 school-funding law is the reporter's, not hers (Spectrum News).
  • Her certification bills and her amendment to a 2023 certification bill are under Voting record.

Social media

  • August 19, 2026: "No governor in Texas history has driven more teachers away from the classroom than Greg Abbott" (Bluesky).
  • May 4, 2026: "the best thing we can do for our kids is put a great teacher in the classroom" (Bluesky).

Higher education

Campaign pledge

A Texas Tribune affordability guide summarized her plan this way: "She wants to appoint university regents who would expand tuition grant programs to include housing, food, transportation and textbook costs"; the guide adds that she would have the Texas Higher Education Coordinating Board, a state higher-education agency, investigate rising costs (Tribune guide republished by HPPR, October 8, 2026).

Statement or interview

In 2016 the Daily Texan reported that she "believes public universities should have the same right as private universities to opt out of campus carry" (Daily Texan).

Other

Campaign pledge

The same guide, under its "Childcare" heading, says she "told the Texas Tribune that Texas' vouchers should be expanded to include public pre-K programs" (HPPR). The guide does not say whether "vouchers" means the education savings accounts or child-care subsidies, and her full answer was not found.

Statement or interview

On October 15, 2026, outside the Governor's Mansion, she named raising the purchase age for assault-style weapons to 21 as a school-safety priority after the Uvalde shooting: "That will be my priority. Out of decency for what was lost." (Houston Chronicle). The event was a debate challenge, and "Abbott did not make an appearance"; no debate took place (ABC News 4).

64 rows

Differences from ATPE's positions.

  • ATPE's vote record lists House Bill 4545 of 2021 as a yes vote (ATPE Teach the Vote); the House Journal shows she voted no on both third-reading votes and yes on concurrence, the final version (House Journal, record vote 1689).
  • ATPE opposed Senate Bill 9 of 2023, the "Teacher Bill of Rights" with a one-time $2,000 stipend, as insufficient; she voted for it on House second reading (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • ATPE supported the 2023 school-safety bill, House Bill 3; she voted against its conference committee report, the final version (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • ATPE opposed the compressed minimum salary schedule in House Bill 100 of 2023; she voted for the bill on House third reading (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • ATPE supported Senate Bill 568 of 2025 (ATPE Teach the Vote); she was absent for its House third reading and voted for its conference committee report (House Journal, record vote 4139).
  • No difference from the positions of Texas AFT or the Texas State Teachers Association was found.

22 rows

Floor amendments she offered.

  • House Bill 621 (2023), a temporary teaching certificate for veterans without a bachelor's degree: her Amendment 1 was adopted on May 1, 2023, limiting the certificate to career and technology education and cutting its validity from five years to three (amendment; House Journal, May 1, 2023). She was recorded as voting no on sending the bill to third reading, then filed a statement: "When Record No. 783 was taken, I was shown voting no. I intended to vote yes. Hinojosa" (House Journal). She was one of the House negotiators on the bill's conference committee (Texas Legislature Online, 88th Legislature). The enacted law covers "creating a temporary certification to teach career and technology education for certain military service members and first responders", valid for three years (enrolled bill). She gave no stated reason for the amendment.
  • Senate Bill 2 (2025), Amendment 18: would have struck the option of "a nationally norm-referenced assessment instrument" so that students using education savings accounts take the state test; tabled (amendment).
  • Senate Bill 10 (2025), Ten Commandments: her amendment providing that "the state is liable for the expenses, costs, judgments, or settlements" of school lawsuits over compliance was adopted; she then voted against the bill on second and third reading (amendment).

Education bills she authored. None of these became law as a standalone bill.

  • Charter admissions, filed every session from 2017 to 2025. House Bill 1179 (2025) would add "discipline history" to barred admission criteria (bill text). House Bill 43 (2019) and House Bill 97 (2021) cleared committee; the last action on each was the committee report being sent to the House Calendars Committee, and neither advanced further. Raise Your Hand Texas, a public-education advocacy group that does not endorse candidates, testified: "Raise Your Hand Texas supports House Bill 97 by Representative Hinojosa" (testimony, April 2021).
  • Pay and funding: House Bill 3804 (2019), an annual cost-of-living adjustment for district employees (bill text); House Bill 4586 (2023), raising the basic allotment to "$7,075" (bill text); House Bill 177 (2023, third called session), a "one-time bonus payment of $15,000" for teachers (bill text).
  • House Bill 5419 (2025): audit TEA contracts, cap its staffing, and set as a funding priority "attaining and maintaining pay for classroom teachers of at least the national average" (bill text).
  • House Bill 613 (2021): would have made the commissioner of education an elected office ("commissioner of education is elected by the qualified voters at the general election") (bill text). Her 2026 pledge is to appoint an educator; no statement reconciling the two was found.
  • House Bill 4839 (2023), educator preparation: would have added to the accountability standards for teacher preparation programs the "quality of a candidate's implementation of the key components of a performance assessment identified by the board"; it did not advance out of committee (bill text).
  • House Bill 1276 (2019), as coauthor: in districts of 5,000 or more students, no student in grades 1 to 6 could be assigned two years in a row to a teacher with under one year of experience who "does not hold the appropriate certificate required under Section 21.003"; it passed the House and did not pass the Senate (bill text).
  • House Bill 5282 (2025): "the agency shall prohibit the use of artificial intelligence" to score written test answers unless validated (bill text).

Austin ISD board votes, 2012 to 2016.

  • December 17, 2012: voted to end the district's contract with the IDEA charter network, which passed 5-4, and against an amendment to keep IDEA's grades 6 and 7 at Allan (Austin ISD Board Briefs).
  • Same meeting: the board, including her, "unanimously approved four Annual Academic and Facilities Recommendations", which included an in-district charter at Travis Heights started by the campus (Austin ISD Board Briefs).
  • October 24, 2016: she moved to replace the post-secondary readiness standard with the passing standard in the district's scorecard; the motion failed (Austin Monitor).
  • No roll call on teacher raises was retrieved. In June 2015, as president, she proposed raises of 3% for all employees in 2015-16 and 2% the next year (Community Impact).

Education-oriented backers

Teacher-organization endorsements. Texas AFT, the Texas affiliate of the American Federation of Teachers, endorsed her for the March 2026 primary and the general election; its executive council approved the endorsement on December 6, 2025, announced December 9 (Texas AFT). The Texas State Teachers Association (TSTA) endorsed her on February 18, 2026 for the primary (TSTA) and kept her on its general-election list (TSTA 2026 list). Her campaign site also lists three local teacher organizations, the Houston Federation of Teachers (the Houston teachers' union), the Alliance of Dallas Educators United Teachers and Aldine AFT Local 6345, without dates (campaign endorsements page).

Teacher-organization money. All are direct contributions unless marked in-kind. The $250,000 from "American Federation of Teachers COPE" on August 5, 2026 came from the national union's committee, not the Texas affiliate (her report); the National Education Association's committee gave a separate $250,000 (her report). Texas AFT's own committee reported $10,000, $25,000 and $50,000 in cash and $9,959 in kind (Texas AFT committee reports, March to May 2026, May to June 2026). The San Antonio Express-News reported on October 8, 2026 that "The national union gave $250,000, while the Texas chapter gave her $85,000" (Express-News).

Statewide executive offices

Vikki Goodwin, Lieutenant Governor

Called by the Associated Press for Goodwin over Dan Patrick, the incumbent (Texas Tribune live results, Associated Press data; Texas Secretary of State unofficial results). Not certified; the canvass ends December 7, 2026.

Background and powers of the officeRecord by issueVoting recordEducation-oriented backersWhat was not found

Background and powers of the office

According to her campaign biography, Goodwin moved to Austin in 1985 to attend the University of Texas and earned a master's degree in public affairs from its LBJ School in 1991 (campaign biography). She is a real-estate broker who owns Goodwin & Goodwin Real Estate (Amarillo Pioneer questionnaire, January 11, 2026). She won House District 47, in western and southwestern Travis County, in 2018 and has served since January 8, 2019 (Legislative Reference Library; ATPE Teach the Vote). She traces her interest in school money to her mother, a Dallas ISD trustee who "would complain that there was little local control over money" (TPR and Texas Tribune, October 15, 2026).

  • Education roles. She never sat on the House Public Education or Higher Education committees. In 2025 she sat on the Appropriations Committee and its subcommittee on Article III, the part of the state budget that funds education (Texas Legislature Online, 89th Legislature). In 2019 she served on the House Democratic Caucus Special Committee on Public Education (archived campaign page, 2019).
  • President of the Senate. The lieutenant governor "shall by virtue of his office be President of the Senate" and casts the deciding vote when the Senate is tied (Texas Constitution, article IV, section 16).
  • Committees and bill referral. Under the Senate's 2025 rules, "All committees and standing subcommittees shall be appointed by the President of the Senate, unless otherwise directed by the Senate"; the President names committee chairs and "shall refer each bill to a proper committee". The Senate's education committee is the "Committee on Education K-16 (11 members)" (Senate Rules, 89th Legislature).
  • The budget. "The lieutenant governor and the speaker are joint chairs" of the Legislative Budget Board, a permanent joint committee of the Legislature, and she appoints three senators to it (Government Code, section 322.001; Legislative Budget Board).
  • Limits. The Senate re-adopts its rules each session. Republicans keep a majority in the Senate, and the Texas Tribune reports that they "only need a simple majority to change to the rules and the authorities they give Goodwin" (Texas Tribune, November 5, 2026).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

Accounts checked: her Bluesky account, @vikkigoodwintx.bsky.social, read in full from November 2024 to November 9, 2026 (429 original posts). Her X account @VikkiGoodwinTX could be read only through posts embedded in news articles (listed above). Facebook, Instagram, Threads and TikTok were not retrieved. All posts below are from Bluesky. No education-specific post appeared between November 4 and November 9, 2026.

Social media

No Bluesky post on teacher preparation or certification was found.

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

In her 2026 ATPE survey she wrote, "I will not approve a budget that provides money for private schools." (ATPE Teach the Vote). In a questionnaire published January 11, 2026 she listed "Increasing funding for public schools and ending the voucher program" among her top three priorities, because "Republicans are sending our tax dollars to private schools and as a result, are severely underfunding our neighborhood schools" (Amarillo Pioneer). The day after the election she told CBS News Texas, "I really want to take the billion dollars that we have going into that ESA program and bring it back into our public schools", adding, "We have a constitutional provision that we have to provide for our public schools" (CBS News Texas, November 8, 2026); she did not describe a mechanism.

  • Her campaign's education page says "Vikki stood with Democrats and Republicans alike to resist voucher schemes for years" and cites her House Bill 3103 under that heading (campaign education page). The bill itself concerns district contracts with charter operators "not located in this state", not vouchers (House Bill 3103).
  • The same page: "Protecting Separation of Church and State – Your tax dollars shouldn't fund religious education." (campaign education page).

Statement or interview

  • March 31, 2018, as a candidate: "Vouchers, if passed, would subtract $$ from public schools" (campaign blog).
  • Signed op-ed, October 12, 2023: "I am strongly opposed to taking public tax dollars and spending them on private school tuition." She argued that fewer teachers would weaken the Teacher Retirement System: "we risk a systematic failure of our Teacher Retirement System" (Austin Chronicle).
  • April 15, 2025, on X, quoted by the Express-News: "We should be putting this up for a vote of Texans. That's how we will truly know whether the people of Texas want vouchers." Gov. Abbott's office replied, "This is how California is run." (San Antonio Express-News).

Social media

  • April 12, 2025, on the House budget: "Ultimately, I couldn't vote for it with the $1 billion for vouchers." (Bluesky).

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

The campaign page pledges to "raise the basic allotment to previous levels, and ensure that future funding keeps up with inflation", citing her House Bill 1770, which would set it at $7,500, and promises "Better Pay for Our Educators" (campaign education page). Spectrum News, in its own words, reported that she would "commit to a 50-50 split between the state and local school districts to fund public schools" (Spectrum News). Her ATPE survey says, "I plan to increase school funding and simplify the funding formula", and "Schools should have discretion on how to spend their funds", giving the reason "The current formula is overly complex, and it was made even more so in 2025." (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

In 2018 she listed recapture among the causes of district cuts ("Rising property values = more $$ going to the state for other school districts") and wrote, "I pledge to use my budgeting expertise to stop sucking funds from our FREE public schools and increase the per student funding from the state." (campaign blog). Her House district includes parts of the Austin, Eanes, Lake Travis, Leander and Round Rock school districts (Texas Legislative Council district table). Her statements on recapture, which she also calls "Robin Hood", changed:

  • March 6, 2019 newsletter: "we can't do away with Robin Hood due to our constitution requiring an equitable education system" (House newsletter).
  • June 6, 2019 newsletter: House Bill 3, which she coauthored, means "all school districts in HD 47 will see a decrease in their Robin Hood recapture payments" (House newsletter).
  • February 11, 2025: "Honestly, if we weren't paying recapture, the state could make up that difference" and "The state isn't paying its share, in our opinion"; she also supported a commission on the formula (FOX 7 Austin).

Social media

  • January 24, 2025: "AISD paid $904M in recapture, or 20% of all recapture paid in Texas in 2023" (Bluesky).
  • May 6, 2025, on House Bill 2 waiting in the Senate: "The Lt Gov has the power, but not the will." (Bluesky).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

The campaign page: "Less Testing, More Teaching – Our kids and teachers are drowning in STAAR test prep that steals valuable classroom time" (campaign education page). STAAR is the state's standardized test. Her ATPE survey: "I will end the state takeover of school districts and change the law so that can't happen going forward." (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

In 2022: "Standardized tests should not be used to determine teacher pay or to assign a grade to a school." (ATPE Teach the Vote). In July 2024 she called for an investigation of Houston ISD Superintendent Mike Miles over alleged diversion of funds to a charter network: "This is unconscionable, and should not be allowed" (Spectrum News). A machine transcript of a September 21, 2026 podcast interview has her saying "we spend too much time on star [STAAR] testing" and "I don't know that I support closing down schools", and that TEA should support districts "as opposed to coming in and replacing a school board" (The Texan Podcast transcript).

Social media

  • April 24, 2025, when A-F ratings were released: "Maybe it's our accountability system, or the TX Education Agency, that is failing our students?" (Bluesky).
  • August 27, 2025, her reason for voting against House Bill 8 on second and third reading the day before: "The goal is to remove the high stakes and cut down on time spent teaching to the test. This bill won't accomplish those goals." (Bluesky).
  • March 6, 2026: "As Lt Governor, I will make sure the state is funding and supporting our school districts, not taking them over. We should not remove locally elected school boards." (Bluesky).

Charter schools

Statement or interview

In 2018 she listed "Charter schools decrease public school enrollment" among the causes of district funding stress (campaign blog). In 2022 she wrote that charters were meant to spread innovation, "This has not happened, and now charters are growing at an exponential rate", and "I filed a bill that would provide additional transparency around new charter school locations, and I am in favor or reducing the number of charter schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote). Her 2026 campaign materials say nothing about charters.

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Her ATPE survey: "I will ensure librarians can do their jobs without fear of retribution." (ATPE Teach the Vote). At a panel after a film screening on book bans, reported October 15, 2026, she said, "Let's go back to that process that we had pre all these book ban bills"; according to TPR and the Texas Tribune, she also said she would push "to end the chilling effect and let school districts know that it's fine to have diversity and equity and inclusion" (TPR and Texas Tribune).

Statement or interview

  • 2022: "The state's role is to fund schools and to provide very broad guidance, but not to make decisions about specific curriculum and instructional materials" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • 2026: "I have opposed the book ban bills since there was already a process for schools to review books when there are complaints." and "I also opposed bills that would limit the clubs allowed on campus." (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • May 2025, House floor debate on the Ten Commandments bill, Senate Bill 10: "it just seems like we're trampling over the freedom of religion by passing your bill" (CBS News Texas).
  • Bluebonnet Learning materials. On House Bill 1605 of 2023, which led to these state-written materials, she voted no on second reading on May 2, 2023 (House Journal), yes on third reading on May 3 (House Journal) and no on the final version returned from the Senate on May 17 (House Journal). The Texas Classroom Teachers Association (TCTA), a teacher association, reported that at the House Appropriations Committee on March 31, 2025 she "had previously expressed concerns with funding for Bluebonnet Learning materials and symbolically voted against the budget as well" (TCTA). Her own words on Bluebonnet were not found.
  • Sherry Sylvester, a former Patrick adviser, said in October 2026: "She is a knee-jerk back bencher whose votes reveal she is a fan of DEI and critical race theory and is fine with … pornography in public school libraries" (TPR and Texas Tribune). The journal shows she voted against the 2023 and 2025 library bills: House Bill 900 on second and third reading (House Journal) and Senate Bill 13 on second reading, third reading and the conference committee report (House Journal).

Social media

  • November 25, 2024, listing signs of underfunding: "Taking bible infused curriculum for $60/student" (Bluesky).
  • March 26, 2025: "Public school teachers aren't the appropriate people to teach religion." (Bluesky).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

No pledge on teacher preparation or certification was found from her 2026 campaign; its campaign pages and questionnaires were checked.

Statement or interview

  • Campaign page: "Too many teachers are leaving the profession, and too few college students are choosing to go into teaching." It gives as reasons "Low pay, excessive paperwork, constant testing and safety concerns" and states no certification policy (campaign education page).
  • 2022 campaign priorities: "Provide more training to prepare our teachers to deliver the best education for our students with disabilities." (archived campaign page, 2022).
  • 2022 survey: "For teachers who work with the population of students needing special education, I will advocate for improved and additional training." (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • A machine transcript of the September 21, 2026 podcast has her saying: "Over half of our teachers now aren't certified. So they don't have the preparation to be as effective as they can be. Maybe we need to make sure that they get the extra training that's necessary so they can handle a classroom". Her figure on the uncertified share was not checked (The Texan Podcast transcript).
  • ATPE's vote record lists her as voting in 2019 for House Bill 1276 (certified teachers in the early grades) and House Bill 102 (mentoring), and in 2023 against Senate Bill 798 (school counselors without teaching experience) (ATPE Teach the Vote). Those votes are not in the journal records compiled for this report.
  • Her special-education certificate bills and her coauthorship of a temporary certificate for out-of-state teachers are under Voting record.

Higher education

Campaign pledge

The Texas Tribune's affordability guide reported that she would "prioritize legislation" that "lowers college costs, including increasing community college funding and expanding college credit opportunities in high school" (Tribune guide republished by HPPR).

Statement or interview

Her 2019 campaign page said her bill would "allow public universities to make the decision whether or not to allow guns on college campuses" (archived campaign page, 2019). On November 5, 2026 the Texas Tribune wrote that she and Hinojosa "campaigned on K-12 education but offered few specifics about how they would change Texas colleges and universities" (KSAT, carrying the Texas Tribune).

Social media

  • October 15, 2026: "I've got a plan to make college more affordable and protect free speech for students and professors." No plan document was found on her campaign site (Bluesky).

Other

Campaign pledge

Her campaign's issues page says "Vikki is working to expand Pre-K eligibility to include children of all public school employees", citing House Bill 494 (campaign issues page); the bill text covers the child of "a full-time employee of the school district" (House Bill 494). After the election she said, "I want to make sure that I have conversations with each and every Senator to understand what their district needs"; she named no education committee chair or plan for the education savings account program (CBS News Texas).

Statement or interview

On the 2023 school-safety bill's requirement of an armed person on every campus, she said "The potential for disastrous consequences is staggering" (Texas Tribune, May 28, 2023). No joint debate took place; according to TPR, Patrick declined (TPR, The Source).

39 rows

Differences from ATPE's positions.

  • ATPE opposed the 2021 bills expanding full-time virtual school; she voted for Senate Bill 15 of 2021 on second and third reading, and wrote in 2022 that virtual school should be "a last resort" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • ATPE opposed House Bill 100 of 2023 over its condensed salary schedule, and Senate Bill 9 of 2023 (the "Teacher Bill of Rights" with a $2,000 stipend); she voted for Senate Bill 9 on House second reading and for House Bill 100 on House third reading (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • ATPE supported the 2023 school-safety bill, House Bill 3 (ATPE Teach the Vote); she voted against it at every stage, and her stated objection was the requirement for an armed person on every campus (Texas Tribune).
  • ATPE's entry for House Bill 1605 records her as voting for it (ATPE Teach the Vote); the journal shows the three votes in the table.
  • No difference from Texas AFT or TSTA positions was found.

Her April 12, 2025 post explains a vote against the 2025 House budget over its voucher money (Bluesky); that roll call is not among the journal records collected for this report.

Education bills she authored. None of her authored education bills became law (Texas Legislature Online, 2025 authored bills).

  • Basic allotment increases: House Bill 2139 (2019, inflation adjustment only), House Bill 1107 (2023, "$6,500"), House Bill 351 (2025, "$7,160") and House Bill 1770 (2025, "$7,500" plus an inflation index).
  • Special-education certificate: House Bill 3628 (2019), refiled as House Bill 1814 (2023), provided that "the board shall establish a special education certificate", with the State Board for Educator Certification setting its exam and preparation-program standards. The 2019 bill cleared committee and did not advance out of the House Calendars Committee; the 2023 bill did not advance out of committee.
  • Charter schools: House Bill 3288 (2019), charter bonds to "be reviewed and approved by the Bond Review Board", the state board that reviews certain public bonds under Chapter 1231 of the Government Code; House Bills 2744 (2021) and 341 (2023), titled as funding for small and mid-sized charters, which cap funding for larger charters and set it at "zero if the school has more than 5,000 students in average daily attendance"; House Bills 438 (2021) and 345 (2023), limiting charter expansion amendments to "Not more than once during each biennium".
  • 2025: House Bill 3102 would exempt from recapture a district whose enrollment decline is "attributable to students participating in an education savings account program"; House Bill 4104 would require the commissioner to "remove the conservator, management team, or board of managers" once a taken-over district meets set standards; House Bills 3103 and 494 are described under Campaign pledges.
  • Coauthored: House Bill 3 (2019, enacted); House Bill 4656 (2023), to "establish a temporary teaching certificate for classroom teachers certified by other states", left pending in a Senate committee.

Statewide executive offices

Nathan Johnson, attorney general-elect

Result: called by the Associated Press for Johnson over Mayes Middleton (R) in an open seat (Texas Tribune live results, Associated Press data); margin of 631,187 votes in the Secretary of State's unofficial count (Texas Secretary of State). Not certified; the canvass ends December 7, 2026.

Background and powers of the office

Johnson is a Dallas trial lawyer at Thompson Coburn and has been the state senator for District 16 since January 2019 (Senate member page), having unseated Don Huffines in 2018 (Texas Tribune, October 8, 2026). He won the attorney general runoff over Joe Jaworski on May 26, 2026 (CBS News Texas). He never sat on the Senate education or finance committees (Senate Education Committee roster); among his committee assignments were administration, business, jurisprudence, water and agriculture, and transportation (2023 committees; 2025 committees).

The attorney general "shall prosecute and defend all actions in which the state is interested before the supreme court and courts of appeals" (Government Code §402.021). Two 2025 laws assign education duties. The voucher law, SB 2, says "The attorney general shall represent the state in any legal action brought that challenges the constitutionality or validity of" the program (SB 2 enrolled text). The Ten Commandments law, SB 10, says "The attorney general shall defend a public elementary or secondary school in a cause of action relating to any claims arising out of a school's compliance with this section" (SB 10 enrolled text). The office also issues legal opinions on request, including to "a regent or trustee of a state educational institution," and examines school district bonds before issue (Government Code §402.042; Education Code §45.005). The outgoing office sued Frisco ISD in February 2024 for "illegal electioneering" (Community Impact) and Round Rock and Leander ISDs in November 2025 over Ten Commandments displays (Attorney General release).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

The accounts checked were Bluesky @nathanfortexas.bsky.social (campaign) and @nathanjohnsontx.bsky.social (Senate office). His X, Facebook, Instagram and Threads accounts could not be retrieved. His education posts on Bluesky include:

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

No pledge on defending the voucher law was found. In the Tribune's runoff questionnaire it was Jaworski, not Johnson, who listed the voucher bill among laws he would not defend (Texas Tribune, April 23, 2026).

Statement or interview

Every statement found from 2018 to 2026 opposes vouchers. In 2018 his top priorities included "Block private school vouchers" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On October 13, 2023 his office said, "A private school voucher program is not fundamental to the function of the state or to the success of public education" (release). In 2024 he answered ATPE: "No. I will continue to work hard to stop the turnover of public education to the private sector" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On January 31, 2025 he wrote that "voucher programs do far more damage than good for students, parents, and education" (campaign journal). On February 5, 2025 he joined a statement in the Senate Journal calling SB 2 a "voucher scam" that "siphons off billions of taxpayer dollars for private vendors" (Senate Journal).

Social media

  • Vouchers. April 15, 2026: "these vouchers would mainly benefit private school kids who can already afford to pay" (Bluesky).

School funding and teacher pay

Statement or interview

In 2019 his office said of property-tax relief and school finance, "Neither can be fixed without the state fulfilling its constitutional responsibility to fund our public education system" (release). In 2024 he favored indexing the basic allotment, the state's base funding per student, to inflation, because a flat raise "would lose value over time" (ATPE Teach the Vote); his SB 233 of 2025 would have raised it "from $6,160 to $7,750 per student" (campaign repost of office release). His 2025 newsletter says three of his bills "passed as amendments to HB 2," including free pre-kindergarten for teachers' children; that is his account of provisions in a larger law. It adds: "The desire to have a family should not deter good people from becoming teachers or from continuing to teach" (2025 Capitol Report).

Social media

  • January 30, 2025: "if we just funded public education at the levels set in 2019, we'd inject an extra $22 billion into the system" (Bluesky). April 29, 2025: "My SB 237 provides educators parental leave after birth or adoption" (Bluesky).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Statement or interview

In 2024 he wrote that test results "should not be used to determine school accountability ratings, teacher pay increases, or student advancement to subsequent grade levels" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On the governor's 2020 waiver of test-based promotion requirements he wrote: "This reprieve from high-stakes testing will help educators give much needed attention" (archived campaign journal). No statement on the Houston ISD takeover was found.

Social media

  • Accountability. March 5, 2025: "Instead of splintering communities, Community Schools are bringing them together" (Bluesky).

Charter schools

Statement or interview

In 2018 he wrote: "Ensure careful regulation of charter schools and use them to as innovation laboratories, not replacements for public schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum and religion in schools. His education pledges concern the Ten Commandments law.

  • CBS News Texas, in an article published April 19, 2026 (the interview date is not given), quotes him: "I will not defend the legislature's passage of a requirement that schools place the Ten Commandments in classrooms because it's unconstitutional." Asked whether the state would need private lawyers, he said, "Yes. If they want to defend unconstitutional laws" (CBS News Texas). SB 10, quoted above, makes defending schools under that law a duty of the office.
  • In a written answer published April 23, 2026, he said: "I believe in carrying out the responsibilities of the office, which includes defending laws I may dislike," adding that this "certainly does not mean or require taking the position that an unconstitutional law is not unconstitutional" (Texas Tribune). In that answer he separates laws he dislikes from laws he considers unconstitutional.
  • KTRE reported on May 14, 2026 that he called the Fifth Circuit ruling allowing the displays "clearly manipulated to reach a political outcome" and, in the reporter's words, would not defend the law while appeals continue (KTRE).
  • Texas AFT, which endorsed him, wrote in a ballot guide dated July 24 (no year given) that he "has vowed not to uphold the enforcement of SB 10 (10 Commandments bill) and 11 (mandated school board vote for school prayer)" (Texas AFT). No statement of his own on SB 11 was found.

Statement or interview

His 2025 newsletter says of SB 10, "The constitutionality of the bill remains in question" (2025 Capitol Report).

Social media

  • Religion in schools. November 21, 2025: "Another judge halted Republicans' law forcing the Ten Commandments into classrooms" (Bluesky).

Teacher preparation and certification

Statement or interview

  • 2018, on the Districts of Innovation law, which lets districts exempt themselves from some state rules, including rules on hiring certified teachers: "an outright exemption strikes me as potentially reckless." He preferred "a 'loosening' of the margins" and said he would "need to become better informed" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • 2024, citing the state's figure that 28.8 percent of new teachers hired in 2022-23 were uncertified: "I will work to provide a clear path to certification, including paid mentorships and apprenticeship, streamlined certification for educators from other states." He also named "meaningful payment incentives for certified teachers, and loan repayment programs for certified teachers" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • In 2023 he offered a floor amendment to keep the State Board of Education's power to review educator-certification rules; see "Voting record."

Social media

  • Teacher workforce. May 25, 2026: "Schools are struggling to find educators to lead career and technical education programs" (Bluesky).

Higher education

Statement or interview

Debating SB 17, the 2023 ban on university diversity offices, he said: "It strikes me as not just ironic or disappointing, but somewhat lazy and remarkably unimaginative" (Senate Journal addendum). The Texan reported that Jaworski criticized his vote for a 2025 bill restricting campus protests, and that Johnson answered that the bill had bipartisan support (The Texan).

Other

Campaign pledge

His campaign priorities page has no education plank and does not use the word "school"; it pledges to "join Democratic AGs from across the country to take Trump to court" (priorities page). At his July 2025 launch he faulted the outgoing attorney general for not joining a multistate suit over "close to $7 billion in education funding," according to the Texas Tribune (Texas Tribune, July 15, 2025). After the election KXAN reported that he promised "a top-to-bottom review of lawsuits and investigations filed by Ken Paxton," with examples that did not include school districts (campaign repost of KXAN).

51 rows

On both 2023 votes on HB 1605 and on the HB 3 (2023) conference report, most Senate Democrats voted the other way (Senate Journal, May 12, 2023; Senate Journal, May 28, 2023).

The 2023 amendment on educator-certification rules. On April 6, 2023, during debate on SB 9 (the Senate's 2023 teacher bill), Johnson offered Floor Amendment 9, which would "strike "(1) Section 21.042, Education Code;"" from the bill's list of repealed laws (Senate Journal). Section 21.042 lets the State Board of Education, the elected board that sets curriculum standards, reject, but not amend, a rule of the State Board for Educator Certification by two-thirds of members present and voting. ATPE describes the amendment as preserving the elected board's veto over certification rules (ATPE Teach the Vote). It failed, 12 to 19. He also voted for Floor Amendment 8, a $10,000 teacher raise, which failed 12 to 19, and against SB 9, which passed the Senate 22 to 9 and did not become law (Senate Journal).

Other votes appear in the Senate Journal and in ATPE's record:

  • SB 1569 (2019), a ban on electioneering by school employees with criminal penalties: on April 17, 2019 he voted no on taking up the bill, then yes on passage to engrossment after amendments and on final passage (Senate Journal). ATPE opposed the bill (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • SB 1962 (2025), on assessment, accountability and suits challenging the school system: no on the Senate floor, April 16, 2025 (Senate Journal).
  • SB 10 (2025): yes on the Menéndez and Eckhardt amendments to add other religious texts, which failed 10 to 20 (Senate Journal).
  • ATPE records that he voted for SB 798 of 2023 (ending the classroom-experience requirement for school counselors) and HB 1468 of 2021 (virtual schools), both of which ATPE opposed; the journal was not checked for these (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Background and powers of the office

Eckhardt was an assistant Travis County attorney, a county commissioner from 2006 to 2013 and Travis County judge (Senate member page). The Senate biography dates the judgeship 2015 to 2020; her own releases say 2014 to 2020 (release, February 13, 2025). She won a special election for Senate District 14 on July 14, 2020 (Legislative Reference Library) and entered the comptroller race on December 8, 2025 after a brief run for Congress (Texas Tribune). She never sat on the Senate education or finance committees; her assignments included Local Government, Nominations, Veteran Affairs and Transportation (2023 committees; 2025 committees).

The comptroller runs the state's voucher program. SB 2 of 2025 created Texas Education Freedom Accounts, education savings accounts that families can spend on private schooling, and placed them under the comptroller (SB 2 enrolled text):

  • "The comptroller shall adopt rules and procedures as necessary to implement, administer, and enforce this subchapter" (Education Code §29.372) (SB 2 enrolled text).
  • The comptroller certifies the organizations that run the accounts, preapproves providers and vendors, and may suspend them (§§29.354, 29.358, 29.3585) (SB 2 enrolled text).
  • "The comptroller shall contract with a private entity to audit accounts and program participant eligibility data not less than once per year" (§29.363); the comptroller also refers fraud to prosecutors, reports annually on matters including the effect on public-school capacity, and makes decisions that are "final and not subject to appeal" (§§29.366, 29.371, 29.373) (SB 2 enrolled text).
  • The comptroller's budget request states program costs, but "the amount appropriated … must be established by the legislature by appropriation for that biennium" (§29.3521) (SB 2 enrolled text). The program's cap for 2025-27 is $1 billion (Texas Public Radio).

The comptroller also submits a sworn revenue estimate before each regular session, and no appropriation bill passes until the comptroller endorses a certificate on it (Texas Constitution, art. III §49a). The comptroller conducts the study that determines "the total taxable value of all property in each school district" (Government Code §403.302). At Governor Abbott's request, the acting comptroller began spending reviews of Austin, Barbers Hill, El Paso and Prosper ISDs in fall 2026 (Texas Tribune, October 6, 2026).

Record by issue

General notes

Campaign pledge

No pledges on testing, charter schools, curriculum or higher education were found.

Social media

The account checked was Bluesky @saraheckhardttx.bsky.social (75 posts, November 2024 to October 2025; none during the 2026 campaign). Her X, Facebook, Instagram, Threads and TikTok accounts could not be retrieved. Her education posts were:

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

She pledged to audit and report on the voucher program while administering it as the law requires.

  • Her campaign site: "The Comptroller can protect our schools & audit the voucher scheme" (campaign site).
  • To The Texan on July 8, 2026: "We need to audit the daylights out of the voucher system to show who's benefiting most, least, and not at all," because "We cannot afford to subsidize a private elite system for the five percent in private school" (The Texan).
  • Written questionnaire, October 12, 2026: "as Comptroller my responsibility is well laid out in the law." She would report "which schools participate, which do not, and what parts of the state have few to no options for utilizing vouchers" (Courier).
  • The San Antonio Express-News quotes her: "I would follow the law, but I would also follow the money vigorously" (Express-News, October 14, 2026). KERA quotes her on the program's budget: "You don’t haul off and double the funding right out of the gate" (Texas Public Radio, October 29, 2026).
  • November 4, 2026, the day after the election: "As the comptroller, my job is to vigorously follow the money and report back to the Legislature if their investment is not panning out." The Tribune adds that she "acknowledged the limitations of her office, saying that any big overhaul must come from lawmakers" (Texas Tribune, November 7, 2026).

Statement or interview

Her February 5, 2025 office release on SB 2 said, "Public dollars should fund public schools" (release), and that day she joined the Senate Journal statement calling the bill a "voucher scam" (Senate Journal). Her 2026 newsletter calls SB 2 a bill "which I strongly opposed" (2026 newsletter). After the election, Fox 26 quotes her: "This is a state subsidizing of a private industry that was doing just fine without it" (Fox 26).

Social media

  • Vouchers. April 25, 2025: "I spoke against SB 2 on the Senate Floor today" (Bluesky). May 8, 2025: "put guardrails on Abbott’s runaway $1B voucher program" (Bluesky).

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

School funding. She told The Texan the state's Rainy Day Fund "is so big that we could pay down the debt of every single school district that’s running a deficit" (The Texan). At a Texas Tribune event in late April 2026 she said, as quoted on the event page, that she would not be "putting a political thumb on the revenue estimate at any time" (Texas Tribune event).

Statement or interview

School funding. On March 22, 2023, debating SB 4, the school property-tax cut, she said, "If for any reason the state is unable or unwilling to pay … it will be a starvation for our public education system," and voted for the bill (Texas Tribune). In 2025: "Texas must fully fund public education, increasing the basic allotment and indexing it to inflation" (release). Her 2026 newsletter says "Austin ISD is the leader in recapture dollars sent to the State of Texas" (recapture being payments some districts send to the state under the school-finance system) and proposes "a discount for districts that submit recapture payments early" (2026 newsletter).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Statement or interview

On October 3, 2025, backing Austin ISD's consolidation plan, she wrote: "In Houston, it took just one school out of 273 campuses with a bad rating for the state to replace the locally elected school board with TEA appointees" (letter). On August 20, 2026 in Beaumont: "Standardized testing is a helpful tool. It’s not the only metric of educational success" (Beaumont Enterprise). On October 5, 2026 she called the acting comptroller's district reviews "political retribution" and "nothing more than the [comptroller] bolstering TEA's favored model of cuts, consolidations, layoffs and takeovers" (Community Impact).

Charter schools

Statement or interview

Her 2020 platform, posted after a charter-school advocacy group announced support for her unasked, says: "I do not support out of district charters receiving public dollars that would otherwise go to public school districts," and "I think there is room for in-district charters like Travis Heights Elementary School"; the page attributes the first sentence to her by name (archived platform). Her 2025 letter says state law "has intentionally created an exodus to less regulated charter schools" (letter).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Statement or interview

In 2023 she offered an amendment to HB 900, the library-ratings law, requiring the Texas Education Agency to report "the disaggregated demographic data of" authors or main characters of rated books; it failed 12 to 19 (Senate Journal). On March 18, 2025 she offered amendments to SB 10 to add or substitute a Jewish version of the text, the Five Pillars of Islam, the Seven Core Tenets of Hinduism and the Noble Eightfold Path; all failed 10 to 20 (Senate Journal). Her reason for voting no: "This bill unconstitutionally establishes the Christian 10 Commandments as the only sanctioned text for mandatory display in Texas public schools" (Senate Journal).

Social media

  • Curriculum and religion. March 28, 2025, on her amendment to SB 24 of 2025 on teaching "the horrors of communism": "we should also include the horrors of other authoritarian ideologies" (Bluesky). April 15, 2025: "not one that dictates religious practices in public schools" (Bluesky).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

No pledge was found. The Express-News reported that she is "particularly concerned about schools that rely on artificial intelligence for instruction and replace traditional teachers with adult guides" (Express-News).

Statement or interview

No statement was found beyond her bills and votes below.

Higher education

Statement or interview

Debating SB 17 in 2023, she told its author: "I think your bill is concerned that we've made too much progress" (Senate Journal addendum). At the LBJ School on March 2, 2026, the Daily Texan reported, she said the voucher program would "make it harder for public school students to get into flagship universities" (Daily Texan).

Statewide executive offices

Benjamin Flores, Land Commissioner-elect

Elected November 3, 2026, over the incumbent, Republican Dawn Buckingham. The race was called by the Associated Press; the returns are unofficial and not certified, and the state canvass ends December 7, 2026.

At a glance
