Background and powers of the officeRecord by issueVoting recordEducation-oriented backersWhat was not found

Background and powers of the office

Flores has sat on the Bay City City Council since 2023 (city posting) and has held no state office; he works as a cybersecurity and compliance consultant (campaign biography). His 2023 council-campaign site (archived May 12, 2024) says he was president of the parent association at Holy Cross Catholic School in Bay City, the private school his daughter attends. His campaign biography says the family moved from Austin to Bay City in 2016 (campaign); his city biography says they moved to Austin in 2016 and to Bay City several years later (city).

The land commissioner heads the General Land Office (GLO) and chairs the School Land Board (Natural Resources Code §32.014), which oversees the lands of the Permanent School Fund (PSF), a state fund for public schools that includes "all income from the mineral development of permanent school fund land" (Education Code §43.001). The commissioner also holds one of nine seats on the board of the Texas Permanent School Fund Corporation, which since 2021 manages the fund's investments (Education Code §43.053). State guarantees of charter-school bonds are approved by the commissioner of education, not the land commissioner (§45.052), and this review found no GLO power over charter schools.

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

All eight original posts on his Bluesky account (benflores4landcom.bsky.social) were read; his X, Facebook, Instagram, Threads, TikTok and YouTube accounts were not retrieved. A March 3, 2026 post put "better schools" first among the aims of "the GLO 2030 Plan."

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. His campaign Q&A, undated, says: "I'll protect the Permanent School Fund from being raided for voucher schemes."

Statement or interview

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. The Texas Tribune wrote on September 18, 2026: "Flores strongly opposes school vouchers, arguing they undermine the constitutional mission of public lands to fund public education and exacerbate inequality." That is the Tribune's summary; no statement in his own words using "constitutional mission" was found.

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

The same page names "Permanent School Fund Optimization" his top priority and says it "could generate up to $825 million more annually for Texas public schools, potentially lowering everyone's property taxes"; the figure is his campaign's estimate. His home page pledges to expand oil, gas, wind, solar and geothermal leasing "to increase revenue benefiting the Permanent School Fund." In the League of Women Voters Houston voters guide he wrote: "I'll responsibly lease state land for every dollar it can honestly return, publish what each acre earns, and send more of it to classrooms."

Statement or interview

In a signed op-ed from about October 2025 he said he would focus on "doing a better job funding our public schools from the state's Permanent School Fund."

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Statement or interview

The same op-ed says: "I believe in freedom of speech, academic freedom, and focusing my energy on doing the best job I can."

Higher education

Campaign pledge

His GLO 2030 Plan, dated February 2026, speaks of lease revenue "that can go to Texas public schools and universities."

Other

Statement or interview

His city biography, archived October 31, 2025, says: "Though Nadia attends Holy Cross Catholic School, Benjamin is a vocal supporter of strong public schools because great classrooms lift every Texas child."

Voting record

Flores has never held a legislative office, and no city-council motion or vote by him on a school matter was found.

Education-oriented backers

  • Texas AFT COPE, the political committee of the teacher organization Texas AFT, endorsed him on its October 1, 2026 list but not its May 26 list. He is not on the list of the Texas State Teachers Association, another teacher organization. His campaign-finance reports to the Texas Ethics Commission through October 24, 2026 show no money from education groups.
  • His endorsements page lists Mothers Against Greg Abbott, a political group whose public identity is broader than schools. No charter or school-choice backing was found.
  • For his opponent: the Tribune guide lists the Charter Schools Now PAC, a charter-school political committee, among Buckingham's endorsers, and the Texas Home School Coalition, a home-school advocacy group, endorsed her (coalition list).

What was not found

  • Teacher preparation and certification. Nothing, after a search of his campaign site, Q&A, op-ed, plan, voter-guide answers and Bluesky posts.
  • No statement on testing, teacher pay, charter policy beyond vouchers, curriculum or school meals.
  • Reported but unconfirmed: that he called vouchers "unconstitutional," and a San Antonio Express-News editorial endorsement.

Statewide executive offices

Clayton Tucker, Agriculture Commissioner-elect

Elected November 3, 2026, over Republican Nate Sheets in an open seat; Sheets had defeated the incumbent, Sid Miller, in the Republican primary. The race was called by the Associated Press; the returns are unofficial and not certified, and the state canvass ends December 7, 2026.

Background and powers of the office

Tucker has held no public office; he lost a 2020 race for Texas Senate District 24 to Dawn Buckingham (KDH News) and a 2021 Lampasas city-council race (KDH News). His campaign biography describes him as a rancher near Lampasas and a former kindergarten teacher in Taipei. He is the current secretary of the Texas Farmers Union, a farm organization (officers page), and in 2023 he was president of the Texas Progressive Caucus, a progressive political organization (caucus statement).

The agriculture commissioner runs the Texas Department of Agriculture (TDA), which "shall administer" the school lunch, school breakfast and summer food service programs among others (Agriculture Code §12.0025); the same chapter sets up a farm-to-school task force on "locally grown food products in public schools" (§12.0026). TDA says the school lunch program serves "more than 3 million Texas children" (TDA). Summer EBT (SUN Bucks) is not a TDA program: the Texas Tribune reported "a $60 million addition for the Texas Health and Human Services Commission to administer such a program."

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. No pledge in his own words was found; his 2023 statement is under the next heading.

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. An October 31, 2023 statement of the Texas Progressive Caucus, which he then led, warned Democratic lawmakers against any voucher deal and quotes him: "No deals for a one-time funding increase. No deals for a chairmanship."

Testing, accountability and state takeovers; charter schools. Statewide executive offices

Jon Rosenthal, Railroad Commissioner-elect

Elected November 3, 2026, over Republican Bo French in an open seat. The race was called by the Associated Press; the returns are unofficial and not certified, and the state canvass ends December 7, 2026.

Background and powers of the office

Rosenthal has represented Texas House District 135 in northwest Harris County since January 2019 (Legislative Reference Library), first elected in 2018 over the Republican incumbent, Gary Elkins (campaign site; Community Impact). He is a mechanical engineer with 25 years in oil and gas, and he did not seek re-election to the House in order to run for the Railroad Commission (Texas Public Radio). He never sat on the House Public Education or Higher Education committees; his assignments included Energy Resources, County Affairs, and Agriculture and Livestock (2019, 2021, 2023, 2025).

The Railroad Commission of Texas regulates oil and gas wells and pipelines (Natural Resources Code §81.051). Production taxes reach schools through the comptroller, not the commission: after other deductions, the comptroller deposits "one-fourth of the revenue collected from the tax imposed by this chapter to the credit of the foundation school fund" (Tax Code §202.353; §201.404). This review found no statute giving the commission authority over schools.

Record by issue

General notes

Campaign pledge

His 2020 pledges are written answers to a survey by the Association of Texas Professional Educators (ATPE), a teacher association that does not endorse candidates, published on its Teach the Vote site. He did not answer its 2022 or 2024 surveys (Teach the Vote).

Social media

All 121 original posts on his Bluesky account (jon-rosenthaltx.bsky.social) were searched; his X, Facebook, Instagram, Threads and YouTube accounts were not fetched. One July 27, 2026 post says "my opponent is attacking Texas seniors, students, and neighbors with disabilities"; it states no education policy.

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. Asked whether he would support any voucher, education savings account or tax-credit program, he answered: "No. Nope. No way." (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

Private-school vouchers and education savings accounts. On October 12, 2023 he said, as quoted by the Houston Chronicle: "Vouchers are a scam that take money out of our schools." The Chronicle reported his view that the money "would be better used to increase the basic allotment and give raises to teachers"; the basic allotment is the base per-student amount in the state formula. On Senate Bill 2 of 2025, which created education savings accounts, he told the Houston Chronicle on April 2, 2025: "It's not a school choice package at all, it's a discount for kids who are already in private schools."

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

On election night in 2018 he told Community Impact: "We're going to address education first and health care second." In the 2020 survey he wrote "Keep the defined benefit pension plan for sure," about the teacher retirement system, and "Teacher pay should be determined on multiple criteria (certainly including seniority!)."

Statement or interview

  • Community Impact reported on June 26, 2019 that "he would like to see more comprehensive school system funding to provide more raises to educators."
  • The Houston Chronicle reported that at a May 6, 2024 Cypress-Fairbanks ISD board meeting on budget and librarian cuts he was "asking every Texan watching to call their representatives and ask for increased funding to public education." He was one of six area House members, in a bipartisan group led by Republican Rep. Tom Oliverson, who signed a letter "urging the Texas Education Agency to release the $30 million from the TEA's surplus" (same report).
  • On May 13, 2024 he and about 40 lawmakers asked Gov. Greg Abbott for a special session; their letter said: "These issues arise from the state's failure to improve school funding since 2019" (Spectrum News). Abbott replied that the signers had voted against a school-funding bill tied to vouchers, according to ABC13.

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

In the 2020 survey he called STAAR, the state's standardized tests, "detrimental to the point of being criminal" and set a goal to "repeal and replace" them: "First steps are to decouple it from school accountability and also further increase pilot programs for alternative testing and evaluation systems." For low-rated schools he wrote: "How about instead we allocate EXTRA resources to struggling schools" (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

On August 13, 2025 he told the Houston Press: "I'm an anti-STAAR crusader," and said he wanted to "revise or actually kill the STAAR test."

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

In the 2020 survey he wrote that "not all charter operations are inherently evil" but that "With today's current budget crunch there is NO WAY that any new charters should be approved."

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Statement or interview

When the Cypress-Fairbanks ISD board removed science-textbook chapters on vaccines, climate change and cultural diversity, he said on June 17, 2024: "As a science-based career professional and a state rep who values and supports public education I'm appalled" (Houston Chronicle).

Higher education

Campaign pledge

His 2026 campaign priority page pledges to "partner with local business and community colleges on paid training."

Statement or interview

In House debate on Senate Bill 18 of 2023, on faculty tenure, he said: "My family came to Texas because of the tenure program at The University of Texas." (House Journal, May 22, 2023).

Other

Statement or interview

Other: oil and gas revenue. He told KTAB on July 26, 2026: "The revenues that come from oil and gas production pay for our schools, our roads, our parks, and so much more."

65 rows

How a teacher association describes some of these votes. According to ATPE's Teach the Vote profile, he voted for two 2021 bills expanding full-time virtual schools (House Bill 1468 and Senate Bill 15), which ATPE opposed, and against the 2023 school-safety conference report and the 2025 discipline bill (House Bill 6), which ATPE supported. ATPE opposed Senate Bill 9 of 2023, for which he voted, "as insufficient in terms of providing a meaningful increase in educator compensation" (Teach the Vote). ATPE also reports that on House Bill 547 of 2021, on home-schooled students' participation in public-school activities, he was recorded as voting yes on concurrence but entered a statement that he intended to vote no (Teach the Vote).

Bills he authored on education.

  • Teacher preparation and certification. He filed the same bill in four sessions: HB 1276 in 2019 (author report), HB 800 in 2021, HB 1005 in 2023 and HB 362 in 2025, each on the assignment of students to uncertified teachers. The 2019 text said that in districts of at least 5,000 students, a student in grades 1 to 6 "may not be assigned for two consecutive school years to a teacher who" has less than one year of experience and lacks the required certificate; parents could consent to the assignment. The 2019 bill passed the House on April 25, 2019 and did not pass the Senate, according to Teach the Vote. He was also a joint author of HB 116 of 2019, on educator training, and a coauthor of HB 1569 of 2023, on training computer-science teachers.
  • Testing, accountability and state takeovers. HB 3545 of 2019 would have replaced a state-appointed board of managers with a "community management board" including "a classroom teacher from the school district or campus." HB 3071 of 2021, with Rep. Garnet Coleman, said "the commissioner may not order the appointment of a board of managers to govern a school district unless at least 15 percent of the campuses" were rated unacceptable. HB 2661 of 2023 would have ended the use of state tests as indicators of achievement in school ratings.
  • Charter schools. HB 1320 of 2021, refiled in 2023 and 2025, would have applied state travel-expense rules to charter schools spending state money; it never passed.
  • School funding and teacher pay. HB 3380 of 2025 would have raised the basic allotment from $6,160 to "the lesser of $7,500" or a formula amount, with an inflation adjustment. He was a coauthor of HB 3 of 2019, the school-finance law.
  • Higher education. HB 626 of 2021, which became law, expanded an adult career-education grant program.

Education-oriented backers

  • Texas AFT COPE endorsed him on its May 26 and October 1, 2026 lists. He is not on the Texas State Teachers Association's 2026 list, and no 2026 teacher-organization money was found.
  • In earlier House races, the association's 2024 list includes him, and its PAC gave him $500 on November 18, 2024, after that general election (campaign-finance report). In 2018 he credited "backing from the Cy-Fair branch of the American Federation of Teachers" (Community Impact). Teach the Vote says he was "Endorsed in the 2022 general election by Texas Parent PAC," a parent political committee.
  • No charter or school-choice backing was found. For his opponent: the Texas Home School Coalition endorsed French (coalition list).

What was not found

  • Teacher preparation and certification. No pledge, statement or post; his record is the bills above.
  • No personal explanation of his votes against the final versions of House Bill 2 and House Bill 8 of 2025, and no statement on the Houston ISD takeover.
  • Reported but unconfirmed: two 2021 letters on virtual instruction and STAAR that he is said to have signed, and scorecards from Young Conservatives of Texas and another group marking him against their school-choice positions.

U.S. Senate

James Talarico, U.S. senator-elect

Result: called by the Associated Press for Talarico over Ken Paxton (R) (Texas Tribune live results, Associated Press data); margin of 1,195,609 votes in the Secretary of State's unofficial count (Texas Secretary of State). Not certified; the state's official count (the canvass) ends December 7, 2026.

Background and powers of the officeRecord by issueVoting recordEducation-oriented backersWhat was not found

Background and powers of the office

Talarico taught sixth-grade language arts at Rhodes Middle School in San Antonio ISD from 2011 to 2013 as a Teach For America corps member, then spent a year at the Harvard Graduate School of Education (Texas Tribune, January 16, 2026). By 2016 he was Central Texas executive director of Reasoning Mind, a nonprofit offering computer-based math instruction (San Antonio Report). He won a special election to the Texas House on November 6, 2018 (Legislative Reference Library) and represented House District 52, then District 50 after his 2022 reelection (ATPE Teach the Vote). From 2019 to 2025 he also worked for Maya Consulting LLC, an education consulting firm, which paid him $83,333.40 in 2025 (Senate financial disclosure).

He sat on the House Public Education Committee in all four regular sessions from 2019 through 2025 (committee roster). In 2023 he also sat on the select committee that took up vouchers, in 2025 he was vice chair of a Public Education subcommittee on academic and career education, and he served on House-Senate conference committees on school chaplains (SB 763 of 2023) and pre-kindergarten teacher requirements (HB 2729 of 2023) (member page).

A senator's education powers are federal. Senators vote on the appropriations that fund the U.S. Department of Education ("No Money shall be drawn from the Treasury, but in Consequence of Appropriations made by Law") and give "Advice and Consent" on officers of the United States, among them the Secretary of Education (U.S. Constitution). The office has no direct authority over Texas teacher certification, the Texas Education Agency (the state agency that oversees public schools) or state school-finance formulas. No Senate committee assignment for him has been found.

Record by issue

General notes

Campaign pledge

The pledges below are from his campaign's public-education page (published January 2, 2026, modified October 12, 2026) unless another source is named (public-education page).

Statement or interview

Quotations are his own words unless marked as campaign or reporter text.

Social media

The account checked was X @jamestalarico, read through posts embedded on his campaign pages and through Thread Reader. His Instagram, TikTok, Facebook and YouTube accounts were not retrieved, and Bluesky and Threads were not checked. The posts found are all on X:

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

The page pledges to "Reject the voucher scams being pushed by billionaire mega-donors that defund public schools to subsidize private education for the wealthiest Americans" (public-education page). His rural page pledges to "Oppose the federal voucher scam being pushed by billionaires like Betsy DeVos," without naming a federal bill (rural-investment page).

Statement or interview

Every statement found from 2023 to 2026 opposes private-school vouchers.

  • April 7, 2023, office release after the House barred state budget money for vouchers: "They have a simple strategy: First, demonize, and then dismantle public education" (Gilmer Mirror reprint).
  • July 2023, at the House select committee's hearing, as reported by the Association of Texas Professional Educators (ATPE), a teacher association that does not endorse candidates: "It’s my opinion we shouldn’t be messing with any pet projects or education savings accounts until we do the $1,000 increase." He asked the Texas Education Agency what federal protections special-education students give up by taking an education savings account, a state-funded account parents can spend on private schooling (ATPE).
  • 2024 ATPE candidate survey: "No. I am against voucher scams in any shape or size and will never vote to defund our public schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote).
  • April 16, 2025, House floor, on SB 2, the bill that created the state's education savings accounts: "This amendment would send this private school voucher bill to the voters of Texas for their approval before it can be implemented," because "private schools can deny admission to any kid for any reason they want." The House tabled the amendment (House Journal).
  • January 24, 2026, primary debate: "I have gone toe to toe with the billionaires who are trying to dismantle public education in this state" (Texas Tribune).

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

At a CNN town hall on October 14, 2026, he said, "One, I would make sure that we fully fund the Department of Education," and "I think we can do so much to finally pass a pay raise for our hardworking educators," naming no federal mechanism for the raise (CNN transcript). KLTV reported on May 29, 2026 that "he hopes to push at the federal level to incentivize states to get teachers above the national average" (KLTV).

Statement or interview

School funding, teacher pay and class size. On April 16, 2025 he said of HB 2, the 2025 school-finance and teacher-pay law: "I voted for that bill in committee and on the floor, but I also made the point on this back microphone that it was inadequate to the crisis that our schools are facing" (House Journal). Explaining why he left teaching, he told the Tribune: "An education system that is built on property wealth is always going to be inherently unequal" (Texas Tribune). At the CNN town hall: "I believe that educators should be sitting at the decision-making table when it comes to making decisions about education policy" (CNN transcript).

His campaign site says he "Overhauled Texas’s school finance system and invested over $6.5 billion into public schools for teacher pay raises, educator training, new literacy and math proficiency initiatives" (public-education page). The legislative record lists him as one of about 140 coauthors of that 2019 law, HB 3, not one of its five authors (HB 3 bill history). He wrote in 2024 that "crafting HB 3 and fixing our broken school finance system, was my top priority" (ATPE Teach the Vote).

Two accounts of his class size exist. He has said he had 45 students in one classroom, on his campaign page (public-education page) and at the CNN town hall: "At one point, I had 45 students in one classroom" (CNN transcript). The Daily Wire, a conservative outlet, reported on October 28, 2026 that "Texas Education Agency documents reviewed by the Times, however, indicate that the largest class period he taught at Rhodes Middle School had 30 students, while most of his classes ranged from 22 to 27" (Daily Wire). The same article quotes campaign spokesman JT Ennis: "After having 45 students placed in his classroom, James worked with school administrators to redistribute students across classes and create the learning environment that his students deserved" (Daily Wire). The New York Times profile that the Daily Wire cites could not be opened for this review, so the class-size figure is reported here secondhand.

Social media

  • January 24, 2023: "Today I’m introducing legislation to give every teacher in Texas a $15,000 pay raise" (X post). May 14, 2021: "I just passed legislation to limit the size of pre-k classes to 22 students in Texas" (X post).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

Testing and instruction. His one pledge here is for grants "to encourage public schools to adopt evidence-based strategies like phonics, early literacy programs, and 1:1 intensive tutoring" (public-education page). He made no pledge on charter schools.

Statement or interview

In his 2022 and 2024 surveys he wrote that tests should not be "the sole, or even the majority, means of evaluating teachers" and that "the current testing regime represents an overcorrection and must be addressed" (ATPE Teach the Vote). Of the state takeover of Houston ISD and vouchers he said on July 13, 2026: "There is a coordinated effort by some of the most powerful politicians, and some of the wealthiest mega donors to dismantle public education" (Texas Metro News).

Charter schools

Statement or interview

He supports nonprofit public charters as public-school choice. In 2022 he wrote: "I believe there is a role for high-quality nonprofit public charter schools in our education system," and that his concern is "not with the number of public charter schools, but with the quality" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On the House floor on April 16, 2025: "we could open more magnets, more academies, more career and technical schools, more public charter schools, eliminate the wait-lists," and "I'm talking about real school choice, which I support and have supported since I got to this body" (House Journal). Seth Rau, who wrote his 2018 education platform, wrote that Talarico "has been very close to the leaders of the Texas charter school movement, and they hosted many of his earliest fundraisers during his 2018 campaign" (SCHOOLED, March 10, 2026).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum and religion in schools. He pledges to "reject policies that force religion into neutral public spaces like schools, prisons, courts, and the military" (freedom-family-faith page).

Statement or interview

  • May 28, 2021, on HB 3979, the 2021 bill limiting how teachers address race: "Is it fair to say that any bill that strikes language condemning racism is a racist bill?" (Texas Tribune via Click2Houston). His point of order against the Senate's amendments was sustained (ATPE).
  • May 2, 2023, committee hearing on SB 1515, a Senate bill requiring Ten Commandments displays: "This bill to me is not only unconstitutional, it's not only un-American, I think it is also deeply un-Christian" (KUTV).
  • May 8, 2023, on SB 763 (school chaplains): his amendment requiring chaplains to be endorsed by an organization the Department of Defense recognizes was adopted; his amendment requiring parental consent before a child meets a chaplain failed, 61 to 76 (House Journal).
  • 2024, to Commissioner Mike Morath about Bluebonnet Learning (the state-written materials that HB 1605 of 2023 led to): "How do you believe teachers will respond to elementary school students when they ask how a human being can rise from the dead?" (The 74).
  • 2025, on the Ten Commandments law (SB 10): "I worry that these kinds of bills will actually create a new generation of atheists who think our religion is more about power than love." The Austin Chronicle places this in an April debate with Rep. Candy Noble (Austin Chronicle); Texas Public Radio describes a similar argument in the May 24 floor debate, where he said, "The separation of church and state doesn't just protect the state. It also protects the church" (Texas Public Radio).

Social media

  • Curriculum and religion. May 28, 2021, on HB 3979: "If kids are old enough to experience racism, then they're old enough to learn about it" (Thread Reader). May 3, 2023: "This bill is not only un-constitutional and un-American, it’s deeply un-Christian" (X post). March 1, 2024: "The 25 largest school districts in Texas all rejected replacing counselors with chaplains" (faith page embed). A May 29, 2025 post embedded on the same page reads: "forcing teachers to post the Ten Commandments violates the most important commandment: love thy neighbor" (faith page embed).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

The page pledges to "Hire more teachers and equip them with what they need to succeed: Expand programs like the Teacher Quality Partnership, the Supporting Effective Educator Development program, and the TEACH grant that fund and enhance teacher training and are helping to close the teacher workforce gap" (public-education page). This is his only pledge on educator preparation. It gives no dollar amounts and does not mention residencies or alternative certification.

Statement or interview

He entered teaching through Teach For America after graduating from the University of Texas. The sources differ on his graduation year: 2011 according to the Daily Texan (Daily Texan) and 2010 according to the Tribune (Texas Tribune). He taught from 2011 to 2013 (Texas Tribune). Which certification program he completed, and his certificate type, were not found.

  • 2024 survey, his one located answer on the supply of certified teachers: "the best way to have adequate numbers of trained and certified teachers is to listen to Governor Abbott's own Teacher Vacancy Task Force which recommended increasing teacher pay." He also named retirement benefits and workload (ATPE Teach the Vote). The answer addresses pay, not preparation or certification routes.
  • At the House Public Education Committee's August 13, 2024 interim hearing on teacher certification, the Texas Classroom Teachers Association (TCTA), a teacher association, reported that he "asked for more details about the survey" (TCTA's member survey on uncertified teachers) and named him among members "particularly supportive of teachers and the teaching profession throughout the hearing." No quotation of his own words on certification from the hearing was found (TCTA).
  • On July 18, 2025, a machine transcript of the Joe Rogan podcast has him saying high-poverty schools give "the kids who have the most troubles to the first year teachers … Almost like a hazing thing" (Rosetta transcript).
  • His educator-workforce bills (the 2021 law changing the composition of the State Board for Educator Certification, which sets certification rules, and a 2025 bill on teachers' appeals under the state's teacher-designation pay system) and his votes on certification and counselor bills are under "Voting record."
  • Rep. Carl Tepper, a Republican, said he "should never claim to be an expert in education because of two years in the classroom" (Texas Tribune).

Higher education

Campaign pledge

The page pledges to improve "junior college and Career and Technical Education programs" (public-education page). The Texas Tribune wrote that he "has called for a reversal of federal cuts to the U.S Department of Education and for additional funding for Pell Grants and other student aid" (Tribune via KSAT, October 8, 2026). FOX 7 Austin, drawing on the campaign and his legislative remarks, wrote that he "advocated for expanding the value of federal Pell Grants, indexing them to inflation" and "supports student debt relief" (FOX 7 Austin, October 21, 2026).

Statement or interview

No statement naming SB 17 of 2023 or SB 37 of 2025, the laws on university diversity offices and university governance, was found; his votes against both are below.

Other

Campaign pledge

The page pledges universal pre-kindergarten "for every 3- and 4-year-old," protection of "federal oversight that ensures special education students receive the funding and support they need," expansion of "the National School Lunch and School Breakfast programs," more school counselors, and federal guidelines "that can help states and schools safely use AI in the classroom" (public-education page).

Statement or interview

He called HB 3 of 2023, which required an armed security officer at each campus, "another unfunded mandate on public schools" (ATPE Teach the Vote). On September 9, 2026 he said, "I will oppose trans athletes playing in girls’ sports if it risks safety or fairness," which the Tribune's headline calls a reversal (Texas Tribune); in 2021 he voted against HB 25, which limited school sports teams by biological sex (House Journal). His campaign page and a 2023 post say he put Narcan "on EVERY school campus" (X post); the law he sponsored, SB 629 of 2023, requires an opioid-antagonist policy "at each campus in the district that serves students in grades 6 through 12" (SB 629 enrolled text).

Social media

  • May 26, 2023: "I just passed a bill to put Narcan on EVERY school campus in Texas" (X post).

Texas Supreme Court

The Texas Supreme Court and public schools

The Texas Supreme Court is the state's highest court for civil cases; its nine justices "get to decide which cases to review, and their rulings … are final" (Texas Tribune guide). Before the election "All of the current justices are Republicans" (same guide); the four Democratic wins "will leave the GOP with a 5-4 advantage on the Supreme Court" (Texas Tribune, November 4, 2026), with Maggie Ellis as chief justice.

School cases reach the court from the intermediate courts of appeals, as the dockets in this section show: suits against the Texas Education Agency (TEA), the commissioner of education and the State Board for Educator Certification (SBEC), which licenses and disciplines educators; state takeovers of districts; teacher contracts and pay grievances; school finance; and immunity defenses of districts and charter schools. Until 2024 most suits against state agencies were appealed to the Third Court of Appeals in Austin, where Ellis, Chari Kelly and Gisela Triana sit; Senate Bill 1045 of 2023 shifted that role when it created a Fifteenth Court of Appeals with "exclusive intermediate appellate jurisdiction" over cases brought by or against state agencies. The law applies to appeals perfected on or after September 1, 2024, and on that date it moved to the new court the cases of that kind pending in other courts of appeals that had been filed on or after September 1, 2023; cases filed earlier stayed in the Third Court.

Candidates for the court are limited in what they may say by the Code of Judicial Conduct, whose Canon 5(1) says: "A judge or judicial candidate shall not: (i) make pledges or promises of conduct in office regarding pending or impending cases, specific classes of cases, specific classes of litigants, or specific propositions of law that would suggest to a reasonable person that the judge is predisposed to a probable decision in cases within the scope of the pledge." Canon 3B(10) applies a similar limit on public comment about pending cases to candidates. This review found no statement by any of the four on vouchers, school finance, the Houston ISD takeover or testing.

As of November 9, 2026, these education cases were pending at the court or could reach it:

  • Morath v. Texas State Teachers Association, No. 25-0527. A challenge to the commissioner's rule for district campuses run by outside partners under Senate Bill 1882 of 2017. Review granted June 12, 2026; argued October 7, 2026; undecided (docket). Ellis joined the Third Court opinion under review.
  • Texas Public Policy Foundation v. Highland Park ISD, No. 25-0759. Whether an exception in the Public Information Act, the state law that requires government bodies to disclose information on request, covers attorney-client material, here a school district's investigation report. Argued October 8, 2026; undecided (docket).
  • Harris County Department of Education Public Facility Corp. v. Paradigm Construction, No. 26-0790. A governmental-immunity dispute over a construction contract, from Kristen Hawkins's district court. Petition stage; the court ordered a response by November 2, 2026, granted in part a motion to extend that deadline on October 14, 2026, and set the response as due December 2, 2026 (docket).
  • Excellence 2000 Inc. v. Texas Education Agency, No. 26-1213. Petition filed October 22, 2026; under review (docket). This review did not read its subject.
  • In re Midland ISD, No. 26-0867. An emergency mandamus petition by Midland ISD against the commissioner of education, denied August 12, 2026 (docket).
  • School ratings. Two suits over A-F ratings ended in the Fifteenth Court of Appeals in 2025 (Kingsville ISD docket; Pecos-Barstow-Toyah ISD docket); no Supreme Court petition was found.
  • No Texas Supreme Court case was found on the 2025 Ten Commandments law or the 2025 education savings account law.

Background and powers of the office

Ellis was "elected to the Texas Third Court of Appeals in 2024" (official biography) and has served there since January 2025 (Tribune guide). Before that she was an administrative law judge at the State Office of Administrative Hearings, a Travis County assistant district attorney and a lawyer in family, juvenile and child-welfare law (official biography). She told Spectrum News on December 10, 2019: "I graduated from UT with a degree in education, then I worked as a teacher." Her court biography lists her as Leander ISD PTA president and vice president and a member of a Leander ISD zoning board, and her campaign site says she is immediate past chair of the State Bar's Juvenile Law Section. A Supreme Court justice votes on which cases the court takes and on how it decides them (Tribune guide).

Record by issue

General notes

Campaign pledge

None on education was found. The Houston Chronicle editorial board wrote of its interviews with her and Blacklock: "Both candidates were circumspect about speaking directly to issues that may appear before them."

Social media

Her accounts on Facebook and Instagram (maggieforjustice) and TikTok (@maggieforjustice) need a login or JavaScript and could not be read. No post was found quoted in news coverage.

Other

Statement or interview

Other: her teaching background. Her campaign home page quotes her: "As a Justice on the Third Court of Appeals, a former teacher, foster mom, and PTA President, I have been a strong advocate for compassion and justice for our community." The campaign biography says "she worked in public education through Leander ISD"; her court biography lists PTA and zoning-board roles there, not employment. She told The Daily Texan on November 21, 2024: "It was really working with kids in the classroom that I knew something wasn't right at home." Her court biography lists "federal education law" among the subjects of her hearings, trials and appeals but names no case or client.

Background and powers of the office

Kelly has been a justice of the Third Court of Appeals, Place 3, since 2019 (campaign site). She was an Army JAG trial defense counsel (campaign awards page), a felony prosecutor in Travis and Comal counties, an assistant attorney general in civil Medicaid fraud and a staff attorney at the Court of Criminal Appeals (Texas Tribune guide). She has taught trial advocacy as an adjunct professor at the University of Texas School of Law for about 20 years (campaign site); no school-board or education-agency role was found. A Supreme Court justice votes on which cases the court takes and on how it decides them (Tribune guide).

Background and powers of the office

Triana has been a justice of the Third Court of Appeals, Place 6, since her election in 2018 (official biography). Before that she "served for 14 years as the 200th District Court Judge" in Travis County (same biography), taking office in 2005; from 1994 to 2004 she was a justice of the peace, an Austin municipal judge and a county court-at-law judge (Texas Tribune guide). Earlier she was a prosecutor in the Travis County Attorney's Office, a staff attorney in the Secretary of State's elections division, and a criminal and family lawyer (official biography). She has served on the Travis County Juvenile Board, the Travis County Child Protective Services Board and the Children's Justice Act Task Force (official biography). She was the Democratic nominee for the same seat in 2020 (Texas Tribune, 2020).

Third Court opinions in TEA and SBEC appeals from Travis County name her as the presiding trial judge (example). A Supreme Court justice votes on which cases the court takes and on how it decides them (Tribune guide).

State (minimum grading)Travis County district court, June 2010 (no cause number found)According to Education Week, "Gisela Triana-Doyal" held that the 2009 truth-in-grading law "is clear that districts can't enforce policies that require a minimum grade for students"Not tracedEducation Week

Teacher preparation and certification. Five of her rulings concern educator licensing: three trial-court reviews of SBEC certificate revocations (Gomez, Lange and Tran, above), the A+ Texas Teachers order on the alternative-certification provider, and her separate opinion in Caddo Mills. In that concurrence she wrote that "the school district's abrupt change in its course of dealing with an educator who had been employed by the district for 15 years in the same position, absent any cause, is the type of unfair surprise that borders on being unconscionable," and called the district's use of "an additional certification that had not previously been required" "disturbing" (docket). These are rulings on the cases before the court, not statements of policy.

Education-oriented backers

No endorsement or money from a teacher organization, parent group or charter or school-choice group was found; the Texas AFT COPE list and the Texas State Teachers Association list include no judicial candidates, and this review searched her eight campaign-finance reports from mid-2023 to October 24, 2026. The Texas AFL-CIO, a labor federation that includes teacher-union affiliates, endorsed her on July 21, 2026 (AFL-CIO list); that is a labor endorsement, not an education one. O'Hanlon, Demerath & Castillo, an Austin law firm whose website lists "School District Social Media Litigation" and "Higher Education" among its practices, gave her $1,000 on November 27, 2023, in an earlier Third Court cycle (campaign-finance report). For her opponent, the Texas Home School Coalition endorsed Busby (coalition list).

What was not found

  • No campaign statement on vouchers, school finance, the Houston ISD takeover, testing, charter policy, curriculum, teacher certification or higher education.
  • The 2010 grading ruling has a second account: one research summary, whose cited Texas Tribune page no longer loads, describes an August 2010 denial of a temporary injunction to the districts. This review could not tell whether that is the same ruling, and no order or cause number was found.
  • Her social-media accounts could not be read.

State Board of Education

The State Board of Education and what it controls

The State Board of Education is the elected board that decides what Texas public-school students are taught and which teaching materials the state approves. It has 15 members, each "elected from districts" (Education Code §7.101). Its powers, with the statute for each:

  • Curriculum standards. The board "shall by rule identify the essential knowledge and skills of each subject of the required curriculum", the standards Texas calls the TEKS, which the law ties to instructional materials and state tests (§28.002(c)). It also sets graduation requirements (§7.102(c)(4)).
  • Instructional materials. It reviews the materials the Texas Education Agency (the state agency that runs public education) sends it and keeps lists of approved and rejected materials; districts may not use rejected ones (§§31.022, 31.024).
  • Permanent School Fund. It "may invest the permanent school fund" under the Texas Constitution (§43.003). Since a 2021 law it has delegated day-to-day investment to the Texas Permanent School Fund Corporation, whose nine-member board includes five State Board members (§§43.052, 43.053).
  • Charter veto. When the commissioner of education proposes a new charter school, the charter takes effect unless, within 90 days, "a majority of the members of the board present and voting vote against the grant" (§12.101(b-0)). The board may not vote on a charter the commissioner has not proposed.
  • Educator-certification rules. The State Board for Educator Certification, a separate state board, must send every rule it proposes to the State Board of Education, which "may reject a proposed rule by a vote of at least two-thirds of the members of the board present and voting" within 90 days but "may not modify" it; a rule not rejected takes effect (§21.042). Those rules cover admission to teacher-preparation programs, the programs themselves, certification exams and certificates, as the board's review of them in June 2026 shows (School Initiatives committee minutes, June 25, 2026).

In 2026 the board passed "sweeping changes to social studies" for kindergarten through high school and "approved a new required reading list that includes Bible stories" (Texas Tribune voter guide, October 16, 2026).

After the November 3, 2026 election, Democrats hold seven seats and Republicans seven, and District 8 is not called (Associated Press results on the Texas Tribune). Results are unofficial until the state canvass, which ends December 7, 2026 (Secretary of State results).

Most board decisions appear in the minutes only as "carried" or as a tally such as 8-7, so a member's individual vote is on record for a minority of decisions (board minutes index). The vote tables below mark the rest "not recorded".

Won a seat held by Republicans; race called by the Associated Press over Republican Victoria Hinojosa (results); not certified.

Background and powers of the office

Garcia teaches English at South Texas College, where he was an adjunct professor and professor in 2022-23 and again since 2024 (Texas Tribune voter guide). The same guide lists him as a teacher in the Pharr-San Juan-Alamo school district in 2016-17, an instructor at UCLA in 2018-20, and the founder and director of a college-access nonprofit, the College Scholarship Leadership Access Program, from 2013 to 2023 (Tribune guide). He has held no elected office and ran unopposed in the March 2026 Democratic primary (Teach the Vote primary results). The seat carries one of 15 votes on the powers set out at the start of this section (§7.101).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

Checked: Bluesky (thomasraygarcia.bsky.social, eight posts read); Facebook, Instagram and TikTok could not be read without a login.

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

Vouchers. His campaign platform page (undated, read November 9, 2026) says: "I am against our state's school voucher program." It adds, "I support school choice in the form of open-enrollment public schools," and gives the reason: "The solution to improving our public schools is to invest more, not to defund them through voucher programs" (platform).

Social media

  • Vouchers and funding. June 3, 2026: "At a time when public education is being underfunded and privatized, I couldn't stand aside" (Bluesky).

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

The Association of Texas Professional Educators is a teacher association that "does not endorse candidates" (Teach the Vote). In its 2026 candidate survey he wrote: "Our state's basic allotment should be doubled, affixed to inflation, and based on student enrollment rather than attendance" (Teach the Vote). His platform says, "I support pay increases for school workers" (platform).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

Testing and accountability. The platform says, of STAAR, the state's standardized tests: "I am against using the STAAR to grade public schools’ accountability scores" (platform). On House Bill 8 of 2025, the testing law: "I do not believe the Commissioner should have the authority to remove or change accountability indicators without consulting the elected SBOE" (Teach the Vote).

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

"Yes, the SBOE should retain its authority to veto or approve new charter applications on a simple majority vote" (Teach the Vote).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum, library books and religion. The platform says: "I am against the state-developed Bluebonnet Learning curriculum," the reading and math materials written by the Texas Education Agency under House Bill 1605 of 2023 (platform). His survey answer is narrower: "I would ensure the Bluebonnet Learning instructional materials are error-free, pedagogically-sound, developmentally-appropriate, and student-centered ... Unfortunately, Bluebonnet Learning is currently none of these things." He opposes the extra state money for districts that adopt the materials ("I believe in local control") and does not pledge to remove them from the approved list (Teach the Vote).

  • On the 2025-26 social studies rewrite, he wants "Much more weight than the SBOE is currently giving educators" and content advisors with classroom experience (Teach the Vote).
  • "I believe local communities should have the freedom to decide what books belong in their own school libraries" (platform).

Social media

  • Curriculum. June 4, 2026: "When we flip this seat in November, I will listen to educators, not extremists, on how to prepare our students for the future" (Bluesky).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

On the board's power over certification rules, he wrote: "Yes. The SBOE is a duly-elected body, and it serves as an important check on the rulemaking actions. The SBOE should also have the ability to amend specific provisions of rules back to the SBEC rather than simply approve or veto rules" (Teach the Vote). Section 21.042 bars the board from changing those rules, so this would take a change in the law (§21.042). His platform targets the Reading Academies, the reading training for teachers that came out of the 2019 school finance law, House Bill 3: "That's why I’ll fight to reform the Reading Academies so they actually help educators improve reading outcomes." His platform says he would phase out "online-only modules in favor of skilled trainers" and seek "funding for math and literacy coaches, math and literacy training for teachers" (platform).

Higher education

Campaign pledge

His platform says: "College for all? No. College access for all? Yes." (platform).

Background and powers of the office

Her campaign describes her as a "27 year Houston ISD educator" (campaign site). The site lists elementary, middle-school and high-school teaching, literacy coaching and testing coordination (campaign site). She now works for the Houston Federation of Teachers, the Houston teachers' union, as "Organizer / Teacher Leader Program Director" (union staff page). She lost the 2022 District 4 Democratic runoff, 41.9% to Staci Childs's 58.1% (Teach the Vote). District precinct chairs chose her for the 2026 ballot on August 15, 2026 (Texas AFT). The seat carries the powers set out at the start of this section (§7.101).

Statement or interview

  • Teacher pay. Written comment on Senate Bill 9 of 2023, May 9, 2023: "We deserve and have earned a raise; not a one-time bonus" and "Many of my colleagues are frustrated and contemplating leaving the profession due to the low pay" (public comments, p. 75).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

Testing and state takeovers. "Dr. Coretta understands that standardized tests, like the STAAR, do not accurately measure intelligence," and "we cannot allow noneducators to make important decisions that govern the direction of our school communities" (campaign site). Texas AFT wrote on August 21, 2026 that she "plans to hold TEA Commissioner Mike Morath accountable for his takeover of Houston ISD"; that is the union's wording, not hers (Texas AFT). In that takeover, announced in 2019, the state put the Houston school district under "an agency-run board of managers" in place of the elected board (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

  • State takeover, charters and testing. A Houston Chronicle op-ed she co-signed on May 3, 2019 said: "A state takeover would lead to irreversible and discriminatory chartering of schools in black and brown communities" and "The charter model is simply incompatible with a public school system committed to serving all students" (Chronicle). In the Chronicle editorial board's 2026 interview she said, "Teachers now are having to fight or mitigate the ills of poverty"; the board wrote that she "had few kind words for HISD Superintendent Mike Miles" (Chronicle, October 10, 2026).

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

In 2022: "I would limit new charter applications. Charter schools received an additional $1,058 per student" (2022 guide, p. 19). In 2026 she said the board can "increase charter school financial and academic accountability" (League of Women Voters Houston guide).

Campaign pledge

Curriculum. In 2022: "The TEKS should be objectively written and research-based by history teachers, so students learn the facts of our history" (2022 guide, p. 19). In 2026 she called for "fact based curriculum materials across grade/content areas, particularly in history and science" (LWV Houston).

Her 2026 guide answer says the board can "fund educator mentor programs to retain educators" (LWV Houston). No other pledge on preparation or certification was found.

  • Teacher training. On Houston's virtual learning, ABC13, September 16, 2020: "I definitely think more training for the HUB is definitely needed" (ABC13).

Voting record

She has never held an office with recorded votes on education policy. In March 2021, as an employee member of the Houston school district's District Advisory Committee, an advisory body, she voted against a District of Innovation plan that included exemptions for "the hiring of noncertified vocational teachers"; the committee rejected it 21-10. She said, "I don’t think the district’s intention necessarily matters when you look at the law" (Houston Chronicle, April 2, 2021).

Campaign pledge

Vouchers. In a Democratic-club questionnaire answer posted on her site (undated), she wrote that public funds, "including vouchers", "must strengthen classrooms, not corporate profits" (campaign FAQ). The answer does not call for repealing the state's voucher program.

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

Testing and accountability. The board's role "should be one of strong oversight, transparency, and advocacy ... Testing should support meaningful learning - not excessive testing or punitive outcomes" (Teach the Vote survey).

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

Her campaign home page says Texas "has allowed unchecked charter growth without proper oversight ... I oppose the unlimited expansion of charter schools that diverts resources from our neighborhood public schools" (campaign site). In the Association of Texas Professional Educators survey she wrote that the board should keep its veto "and the Board should also have a role in reviewing significant charter expansion requests," and that "Charter schools are part of Texas’s public education system" (Teach the Vote).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum, library books and religion.

  • State review materials "should serve as optional tools, not prescriptions"; she does not name Bluebonnet Learning (Teach the Vote).
  • She calls "book bans, curriculum fights, and one-size-fits-all rules" state overreach (campaign FAQ).
  • "Curriculum may teach about religion’s role in history and culture but should not promote or favor any belief system" (LWV Houston, p. 3).

Statement or interview

  • Curriculum and charters. After the board's April 2026 social studies and reading-list votes, she told the Austin Chronicle, "They’re moving away from historically accurate curriculum, fact-based instruction"; asked for the most pressing problem, "Bush argued it’s charter schools" (Austin Chronicle, May 7, 2026).

Statement or interview

  • Curriculum. KUT, October 15, 2026: "I'm seeing that the state board and what they're passing is telling our students what to think and not teaching them how to think" (KUT).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

"The SBOE can strengthen teacher support by setting rigorous, practical preparation standards, protecting certification pathways, and ensuring ongoing professional development aligned to classroom needs" (LWV Houston, p. 3). On the board's review of certification rules: "Yes, the State Board of Education should retain its authority to review and, when necessary, veto SBEC rulemaking. This oversight helps ensure certification policies are fair, transparent, and supportive of educator professionalism and retention" (Teach the Vote). Her home page, as quoted in the Tribune guide, says she "will fight for policies that reduce paperwork, provide resources, improve retention, and restore dignity to the teaching profession" (campaign site).

Campaign pledge

Curriculum. "Science standards need to include climate change for instance" (2026 survey, Teach the Vote). Her undated platform page, which still describes the 2022 race, says "Moses should not be mentioned in the US History curriculum" and supports "actively teaching consent" (platform). On state-written materials she wrote, "I use OER materials as my textbook in all of my courses and none of them are TEA developed" (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

  • Sex education. OutSmart, October 2020: "Abstinence-only sex education is failing the students of Texas" (OutSmart).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

On the certification board's rules, 2026: "SBOE is elected whereas SBEC is appointed. I will always choose the more accountable and pro-democracy way to do things" (Teach the Vote). In 2022 she wrote that educators and groups such as the association "should be met with regarding who to appoint to curriculum committees, revising of teacher certification requirements, and revising of graduation requirements," and gave as a reason for opposing charters that they "do not require teacher certification and do not give yearly contracts" (Teach the Vote). In 2026 she said the board "should help to improve certification pathways to increase the number of trained and certified teachers in TX" (LWV Houston).

Statement or interview

  • Teachers. Texas Tribune, October 22, 2026: "Teachers see what's happening, and they're kind of sick of it" (Tribune).

Voting record

Palmer has never held an office with recorded votes.

Education-oriented backers

Teacher organizations. Texas AFT endorsed her on July 24, 2026 (Texas AFT); the Texas State Teachers Association endorsed her for the general election (TSTA list).

Labor endorsement. The Texas AFL-CIO, the state labor federation, endorsed her in the same announcement (Texas AFT); that is a labor endorsement, not an education one.

Money, November 2026 general election.

  • Texas AFT COPE, $5,000: payer report October 5, her report October 2; the dates differ (payer; her report).
  • Texas State Teachers Association PAC, $250: payer August 13, her report October 2 (payer; her report).
  • Earlier cycle: iVoterGuide lists money she received in 2020 from the Association of Texas Professional Educators (iVoterGuide).

Advocacy groups. Community Voices for Public Education (CVPE) and the Texas Freedom Network (TFN slate) endorsed her.

Charter or choice groups. None for Palmer. Her opponent, Nelson, received a $2,500 contribution and $32,483 in in-kind digital ads from Charter Schools Now PAC (report; report) and in-kind texting from Texans for Educational Freedom ($90,387, including the texting, per the Tribune guide).

What was not found

Searched without result: statements naming Bluebonnet Learning or the 2025 voucher law, the Permanent School Fund, uncertified teachers apart from the 2022 charter remark, alternative certification, teacher residencies, and the Houston takeover. Two audio interviews, from 2022 and September 16, 2026, were not transcribed (Off the Kuff). Reported but not confirmed: her 2020 and 2022 vote shares.

Tiffany Clark, State Board of Education, District 13 (re-elected)

Re-elected; race called by the Associated Press over Republican Carlos "Charlie" Garza (results); not certified. An Association of Texas Professional Educators post after the March primary named a different Republican opponent, April Moore (Teach the Vote); this review did not resolve the difference.

Background and powers of the office

Clark was a middle- and high-school counselor for 10 years and served on the DeSoto school board from 2017 to May 2023, as vice president in 2021-2022 (DeSoto ISD). She took her board seat on January 1, 2025, for an unexpired term, and is an adjunct professor at the University of North Texas at Dallas, "where she teaches future educators" (board biography). She sits on the Committee on School Initiatives, the committee that reviews every certification-board rule and the charter applications (January 2025 minutes). The office's powers are set out at the start of this section (§7.101).

Record by issue

General notes

Campaign pledge

Her campaign home page (undated) pledges to "Advocate for a balanced curriculum that includes an improved testing instrument aligned with Texas standards," to "Fight for the mental health of students, staff, and teachers," and "STOP BOOK BANS!" (campaign site). She did not answer the 2026 Association of Texas Professional Educators survey (Teach the Vote), and the 2024 League of Women Voters guide lists her as unopposed with no answers (2024 guide). No pledge on vouchers, charters or teacher preparation was found.

Social media

Accounts listed on her site (Facebook, Instagram, Threads and TikTok) and her campaign newsletters were not retrieved; no posts were read.

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Statement or interview

Testing and teacher preparation. In written comment for the House Public Education Committee's August 13, 2024 hearing on teacher certification, she wrote that teachers take on debt for more education without more pay and that "it takes more than a test to define a student or teacher. When we talk about teacher certification it has to go back to standards at the home..." (public comments). She also wrote that STAAR "isn't relatable to all students" (public comments).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Statement or interview

Curriculum and religion.

  • Board debate on the social studies draft, April 10, 2026: "It seems as if, when it comes to Black and brown information being in these TEKS, we continue to undermine our experiences" (Texas Tribune).
  • CNN, June 2026, on the reading list with Bible passages: she said she and some Christian constituents believe "Bible lessons should be taught on Sundays," and "Teachers know what their students need... Our students need to focus on reading comprehension" (CNN).
  • November 2024, before she was seated: the Texas Freedom Network reported that "Clark said she would've voted against the curriculum," meaning Bluebonnet Learning (TFN).

Voting record

On charters she voted against allowing every applicant on a named roll call in 2025 and 2026 except Unidos Soccer Leadership Academy, which she voted to allow in committee on June 25, 2025 and then voted not to veto on June 27, 2025; she voted for vetoing Valenta Academy and was absent for the committee vote on the 2026 charter list. She voted no on every recorded vote on the social studies standards (two of them as reported by the Tribune) and on the 2026 reading list. On the American Indian/Native Studies course she voted yes in committee, no on postponing it and yes on suspending the rules to take it up. On certification rules, as the committee member who made the motions, she let two packages take effect. Certification-rule decisions before 2025 predate her service.

33 rows

As a DeSoto school board member. On August 30, 2020, as the district faced possible state oversight, she voted against accepting the superintendent's resignation agreement and against naming an acting superintendent; both carried 4-3 (Dallas Morning News). The district credits her with adding "African-American and Mexican American studies courses to the district curriculum" (DeSoto ISD).

Education-oriented backers

Teacher organizations. Texas AFT's political committee endorsed her for the March 2026 primary round and the general election (May 26 list, archived; October 1 list, archived); the Texas State Teachers Association endorsed her for the general election (TSTA list).

Money.

  • Texas American Federation of Teachers, $250, September 18, 2024, November 2024 general election (her report).
  • Texas State Teachers Association PAC reports $1,000 to her on February 19, 2026 (March primary) and $500 on August 13, 2026 (general); neither appears in her own reports through October 24, 2026, and this review did not establish why (PAC report; PAC report).

Advocacy groups. The Texas Freedom Network endorsed every Democrat on the board ballot (TFN). Charter or choice groups: none found. Her campaign endorsements page is empty. No education group was found backing her opponent.

What was not found

Searched without result: any statement on the 2025 House Bill 2 certification rules or teacher residencies; statements on vouchers or charters; a 2024 platform; her named votes on DeSoto items directed by the state conservator; her campaign newsletters and two 2026 television segments, which were not retrieved. Reported but not confirmed: quotations said to come from WFAA and the Houston Chronicle.

Dana Van de Walker, candidate, State Board of Education, District 8

Race not called. She led the Republican incumbent, Audrey G. Young, by 569 votes in Associated Press data on November 9, 2026 (AP data); the incumbent has not conceded (Texas Tribune). The Houston Chronicle reported on November 5 that the margin is within the range for a recount if Young requests one (Chronicle).

Background and powers of the office

Van de Walker is "Honors and International Director" at Lone Star College (college directory), where the Houston Chronicle says she teaches government; the paper called her "a first-time candidate" (Chronicle, February 15, 2026). Her campaign says she taught English as a second language in Korea and "earned her CELTA teaching certificate," and holds an Ed.D. in higher education leadership (2021) (campaign site). She graded STAAR exams for ETS, a testing organization, from 2015 to 2018 (Teach the Vote). No Texas K-12 classroom job or Texas teaching certificate is stated. The District 8 seat carries the powers set out at the start of this section (§7.101).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

Checked: her Facebook campaign page, Instagram and TikTok (dana4txsboe8); none could be read directly, so no posts were read.

School funding and teacher pay

Campaign pledge

School funding and teacher pay; state takeovers. "I support an across-the-board increase in teacher salaries and an end to state school takeovers" (League of Women Voters Houston guide).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Campaign pledge

Testing. "This experience taught me that we need greater oversight in our standardized testing AND that we need to develop more holistic approaches to student evaulation" (Teach the Vote).

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

In her survey answer she wrote that she would keep the board's veto and limit charter expansion "as they have historically been held to lower accountability standards," while keeping charters as "an option for providing a highly-specialized curriculum, such as foreign language immersion" (Teach the Vote).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum, library books and religion. "I believe that religion has no place in our public schools and will fight to remove the Ten Commandments and any religiously-influenced curriculum ... I am also fiercely opposed to book bans" (Teach the Vote). In her survey answers she wrote that she favors state approval of common materials "by subject-matter experts" and would have "only 1-2 books" state-mandated per grade (Teach the Vote; LWV Houston).

Statement or interview

  • Curriculum. Houston Chronicle, November 5, 2026, after the reporter summarized members' plans to amend the 2026 social studies standards, revisit the reading list and reevaluate Bluebonnet Learning: "We’ve been running with that as a day one promise ... We’re really committed to keeping the church out of our state and also uphold academic integrity" (Chronicle). In February the Chronicle's editorial board wrote that she is "far more worried about the subpar quality of the Bluebonnet curriculum" (Chronicle). Her opponent, Young, answered her criticism of the standards in October: "It’s always great to handpick one or two standards that you don’t like and inflame the information" (Chronicle, October 10, 2026).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

"It is squarely the responsibility of the SBOE to ensure that our teachers are prepared to be in the classrooms ... Alternative certification programs, while useful, should be the exception, not the rule, in teacher certification. Similarly, I believe that all schools under the purview of the SBOE, including charter schools, need to have certified teachers in the classroom" (Teach the Vote). In the 2026 general-election guide: "Texas has a teacher crisis. We have too few teachers overall, along with an unacceptable percentage of teachers coming into our districts without certification (or with alternate certification still in progress)" (LWV Houston).

Statement or interview

  • Teachers. Livingston, July 9, 2026: "A huge priority of mine is to recruit teachers, pay teachers and keep them here in Texas" (Polk County Enterprise).

Higher education

Campaign pledge

Her home page lists "AFFORDABLE HIGHER EDUCATION FOR ALL" with "SEPARATION OF CHURCH AND STATE" and "INVESTMENT IN TEXAS PUBLIC SCHOOLS" (campaign site).

Voting record

Van de Walker has never held an office with recorded votes.

Education-oriented backers

Texas AFT endorsed her on July 24, 2026 (Texas AFT), and in the same announcement the Texas AFL-CIO, the state labor federation, gave her a labor endorsement, not an education one; the Texas State Teachers Association endorsed her (TSTA list), and its PAC gave $250 on August 13, 2026 for the general election (PAC report; her report). The Texas Freedom Network endorsed the full board slate (TFN slate). Her own endorsements page lists no education organization; no charter or choice backing was found. Young received $5,000 from Charter Schools Now PAC on April 28, 2025, plus later contributions and in-kind help (report), and in-kind texting from Texans for Educational Freedom (report).

What was not found

Searched without result: a Permanent School Fund statement, vouchers in her own words, teacher residencies, and the Houston takeover by name. Reported but not confirmed: a 2026 primary-guide answer calling for "routes to alternate certification that actually work" and an end to vouchers (the guide could not be opened), and criticism of her by Texas Scorecard and the Montgomery County Eagle Forum.

Marisa B. Pérez-Díaz, State Board of Education, District 3

Not on the 2026 ballot; term runs to January 1, 2029 (board biography).

At a glance

Private-school vouchers
Voted for a 2022 board priority to "reject all attempts to divert public dollars away from public schools" (minutes)
School funding and teacher pay
Nothing found
Testing, accountability and takeovers
2019: questioned the commissioner on returning Houston ISD to an elected board, per ATPE
Charter schools
Mixed: 2018 called for "a halt to the expansion"; voted to allow several charters, 2019 to 2025
Curriculum, library books and religion
Moved to remove Bluebonnet Learning reading materials from consideration, 2024 (failed 7-8) (minutes); voted against keeping them on the approved list and for keeping the Bluebonnet math materials (minutes)

2018: board's veto over certification rules "should remain intact"

One of 15 board votes; vice chair of the School Finance/Permanent School Fund committee in 2025 (minutes)

None found
Background and powers of the officeRecord by issueVoting recordEducation-oriented backersWhat was not found

Background and powers of the office

Pérez-Díaz was elected in November 2012, took office in 2013 and was re-elected in 2014, 2018, 2022 and 2024; her board biography calls her "the most senior Democrat on the board" (board biography). She worked at the Texas Department of Family and Protective Services, spent "nearly a decade as a district-level administrator in several Texas school districts," and is now a vice president at Firstmark Credit Union. She served as Marisa B. Perez until early 2017 (board biography). She was vice chair of the Committee on School Initiatives, which reviews certification rules, from 2017 to 2020; she has served on the School Finance/Permanent School Fund committee since 2021 and was a founding director of the Texas Permanent School Fund Corporation (board biography). Committee minutes list her as that committee's vice chair in 2025 (minutes). The office's powers are set out at the start of this section (§7.101).

Record by issue

General notes

Social media

No social-media accounts for her were established, and no posts were read; the association's profile lists only marisabperez.com, now a parked page.

Vouchers and education savings accounts

Campaign pledge

Vouchers. Her 2015 campaign page, written in the third person, says that as a new member she "passionately debated and supported the State Board of Education's Anti-Voucher Resolution"; the resolution's date was not checked (archived campaign page).

Testing, accountability and state takeovers

Statement or interview

Testing and state takeovers. In November 2019, after the Houston takeover was announced, she "questioned Morath regarding the process for transitioning from an agency-run board of managers back to a locally elected body," according to the association's account (Teach the Vote).

Charter schools

Campaign pledge

In her 2018 Association of Texas Professional Educators survey: "Overall, the performance of charter schools is unimpressive... It would be my desire to see a halt to the expansion of additional charter schools, until a proper assessment can be made..." (Teach the Vote).

Curriculum, library books and religion in schools

Campaign pledge

Curriculum. The same survey calls the standards "mile wide and inch deep" and says educators "must have a seat at the table" (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

Curriculum and religion.

  • Signed op-ed, San Antonio Express-News, September 15, 2022, after the board delayed the social studies rewrite: "On Sept. 2, the State Board of Education, or SBOE, failed our educators and students" and "We, the SBOE, are the gatekeepers. We should never be the groundskeepers" (Express-News).
  • Bluebonnet debate, November 2024: "Had it not been for the Christian bias in this content, I do really find value in how a lot of these lessons are laid out" (FOX 7 Austin). The association reported that she raised a "conflict of interest" in the agency being both a vendor and a reviewer of materials (Teach the Vote).
  • High-school social studies, August 2026: "Slavery is foundational to this country" (Tribune).

Teacher preparation and certification

Campaign pledge

Asked whether the board should keep its power over certification rules: "Absolutely! SBEC is an appointed board and therefore is answerable to the Commissioner of Education. As an elected body, the SBOE has a responsibility and is accountable to our constituents... the authority to review and veto rule actions should remain intact" (Teach the Vote). She did not answer the association's 2022 or 2024 surveys (Teach the Vote).

Statement or interview

  • After the board rejected edTPA, a performance assessment the certification board had adopted in place of the existing certification exam, she sat for the board on a July 21, 2022 work session with the certification board; the association reported that she "noted that Option C was most aligned with the direction the SBOE wanted to take," Option C being a performance assessment run as a preparation-program requirement rather than a certification exam (Teach the Vote).
  • January 2025: "Responding to a question from Perez-Diaz, Morath clarified there is no requirement that newly hired uncertified teachers ever get certified," in the association's account (Teach the Vote).

Voting record

Charter votes and her 2018 statement, side by side. In 2018 she wrote that she wanted "a halt to the expansion of additional charter schools, until a proper assessment can be made" (Teach the Vote). She voted to allow Gathering Place (2019), Essence Prep and Rocketship (2021), Celebrate Dyslexia and Up Excellence (2023), and Mater Academy San Antonio, Fort Worth STEAM Academy and Frank Liu Jr. Academy (2025), and voted to veto others, as the table shows. No explanation in her own words was found.

Three other named votes differ from those of other Democrats on the board. She voted to keep Bluebonnet Math on the approved list, which passed 13-2 with Democrat Ortega voting no (minutes). She voted for a 2023 change to the materials rubric's abortion language, from "describe" to "promote or encourage," while Democrats Bell-Metereau, Childs and Ortega abstained (minutes). In 2014 she voted to reject a certification rule that Democrats Cortez and Dominguez voted to keep (minutes).

For Royal Public Schools in 2020, the minutes list her among the no votes on allowing the charter (minutes). A table by the Texas Association of School Administrators, a school administrators' group, reportedly shows a different position for her ("Allow"); that table was not read for this review (TASA).
